#6716. 11 Best Cowboy Boots in 2026, According to GQ Editors

You might think that cowboy boots are a privilege reserved for ranch hands and folks who can actually ride a horse. But with a little chutzpah, the best cowboy boots are just as adept on a saddle as they are on the subway. Is that so surprising? Before they blossomed into their fashionable forms, much of menswear’s most riffed on garments were rooted in function: cargo pants, work jackets, even suits. With enough time, they were adopted by civilians and adapted to their second lives as symbols of self-expression and creativity, not just job uniforms. Cowboy boots are no different.

In terms of styling, the classic move for western boots is to lean into their rougher side. Toss them on with some well-fitting jeans (preferably vintage boot cuts) and with a snap shirt. And that's still a great approach. But if you open up your mind and step outside of the saloon, the possibilities are practically endless. A slick pair of exotic leather cowboy boots matched with a ‘70s-inspired suit screams filthy rich oil tycoon if not Alessandro Michele-era Gucci. Or, why not mix up a preppy pair of chinos with a grungy graphic tee and some roughed-up suede ropers? The point is, you don’t have to pretend to be a cowboy to wear their boots. So treat them like any other pair of shoes in your closet and wear them however you want. Hell, we’d go so far as to call them an essential pair of boots for every wardrobe.

And if you’re having trouble, well, that’s why you’re here. We’ve wrangled together the best cowboy boots west—and east—of the Mississippi, from the budget-friendly to the ostentatious and every vibe in-between.

The good folks at Tecovas are living, breathing proof that direct-to-consumer brands aren’t all operated by algorithms tasked with solving imaginary problems. The DTC upstart might be the youngest of the bootmakers on this list, but it's quickly endeared itself to budget-strapped swashbucklers with a hankering for democratically-priced kicks.

Take, for example, the Cartwright, a handsome riff on a classic western silhouette made in Leon, Mexico, a bona fide cobbling capital of the world with over a century of expertise to draw on. It boasts hand-laid cording at the shaft, a resoleable Goodyear-welt construction, and a slew of colors and leathers to choose from, including genuine cowhide and goat leather. They’re not the cheapest boots on this list, but the relatively approachable price belies the craftsmanship involved. For $375, they’re one small step up from our favorite budget cowboy boots—but one giant leap in overall quality.

You can think of roper boots as the chilled-out version of the average western boot. Originally designed for calf roping, roper boots feature a shorter shaft and a shorter, squared off heel. These details made it easier for cowboys to dismount their horse and run after the calves they roped. Coincidentally, they also make roper boots easier to walk in which is why some people prefer them over other cowboy boots for everyday wear.

Our pick for the best roper boots currently goes to Red Wing. (Perhaps no surprise, given the brand also makes our favorite trend-proof boots.) Part of Red Wing's Heritage line, they're crafted with premium S.B. Foot Tanning Company leather, and feature Goodyear welt construction so you can have them resoled and keep ‘em going for a long time. Plus, if you’re just dipping your toes into the cowboy boot world, the minimalist uppers forgo the decorative western stitching which makes them easier to style.

If you’re after that western look but need a pair of boots that can handle the demands of a jobsite, a lot of guys will tell you to look to Ariat. That’s because they manage to blend classic cowboy style with modern-day features, and a palatable price point in perfect proportion. While they do offer a range of work boots in lace-up styles, their Groundbreaker boots are a standout with classic cowboy boot looks.

They’re made of full-grain leather, include a supportive and comfortable insole, and meet ASTM standards for slip resistance and electrical safety. Because it’s among Ariat’s most popular styles, it’s also available in a wide range of colorways, toe shapes, and even steel toe versions. Some more traditional cowboys will poo-poo the fact that these aren’t made stateside, but if you’re on a budget, you’ll get more than what you pay for.

Cowboy boots aren’t all rough-and-tumble affairs. In fact, the right pair can easily strut the red carpet or waltz into a fancy restaurant with panache. You’ll want a pair with leather that shines up well and in a darker colorway. For date nights and more formal affairs, these Tony Lamas will impress thanks to the exotic lizard skin uppers and low-key embroidery. They’re the kind of boots that can play well for the in-laws with a tailored suit or play a rock-’n’-roll star with some vintage jeans and a tight white tee.

These days, most boot brands walk in the shadows of one brand: Lucchese. They’ve been outfitting American presidents, high-profile celebs, and the Dallas Cowboy Cheerleaders for decades and it’s all thanks to their commitment to high-quality craftsmanship. Though any pair of Lucchese’s would earn you more than a few approving head nods, the most coveted are the boots that they make in their Texas workshop.

Using a range of full-grain leathers and exotic leathers like caiman alligator and pirarucu fish skin, each pair is crafted by master bootmakers using time-honored techniques. The Gavin, in their iconic Black Cherry, is a picture-perfect style featuring smooth Santini goat leather uppers with intricate embellishments, welted construction, and lemonwood pegs—made the old-fashioned way, basically. They’ll cost you a pretty penny, of course, but they'll last pretty much forever, and the drip you’ll get in return is priceless.

From Ralph Lauren’s western-inspired sub-label, these RRL boots meld high-end materials with old-world craftsmanship and feature American-tanned leather uppers, Goodyear-welted construction, and vintage-style rubber soles—all crafted in a multi-generational Texas bootmaking factory.

Frye is another American brand with proven boot quality chops. (In fact, it claims to be America's oldest continually-operating boot company.) Its Roper boot has all the same strengths as Red Wing's, and comes in a non-roughout leather.

We don't typically include the same designers twice on one list, but Tecovas are that good. The Johnny is for anyone who wants a softer, slightly more charming boot than the eternal Cartwright up top.

For fellas whose style skews a little more regular-guy than rodeo, these lower-profile, lower-priced Ropers have a Goodyear welt and Vibram outsole. Bang for your, ahem, buck.

Wythe's playing-card-inspired boots feature all the bells and whistles, including premium leather inside and out, hand-applied details, lemonwood pegs, and second-to-none construction all-around. While some brands tap the expert bootmakers in Léon, Mexico to pump out high-quality mass-appeal boots, Wythe tapped these artisans to really let them cook.

All of Yuketen's shoes are perfect, which is a big part of the reason Drake's now carries them. And while they're best known for their moccasin styles, the brand makes an exceptionally handsome cowboy boot too. Really, they're just showing off now.

The classic move for western boots is to lean into their rougher side. Toss them on with well-fitting jeans (preferably vintage bootcuts) and a snap-button shirt. But open up your mind and step outside of the saloon and the possibilities are practically endless. A slick pair of exotic leather cowboy boots matched with a '70s-inspired suit screams filthy-rich oil tycoon—if not Alessandro Michele-era Gucci. Or why not mix up a preppy pair of chinos with a grungy graphic tee and some roughed-up suede ropers? The point is, you don’t have to pretend to be a cowboy to wear their boots (though you certainly can). So treat them like any other pair of shoes in your closet and wear them however you want. Hell, we’d go so far as to call them an essential pair of boots for every wardrobe.

Peter Middleton knows a thing or two about the lifestyle having cut his teeth at Ralph Lauren and used it as a springboard for his own western-inspired brand, Wythe. “To me the beauty and shape of the Cowboy Boot (and a lot of the original design intent) lies in its curves and that’s what makes a great boot SEXY. There isn't a lot of men's footwear that has these dramatic curves, leather, and a high heel but the right cowboy boot has them all.”

He says that good cowboy boots should be made with natural leathers that age well with wear, mould to the wearer’s feet, and can be recrafted repeatedly. His non-negotiables include full leather construction from the insole to the outsole, linings, uppers, and heels. Look for Goodyear welted construction which will allow you to have the boots resoled and extend their life. The toe bug (the fancy stitching at the toe), should sit right where your foot flexes to help cover up the crease that comes with each step. Preferably, this would be corded by hand, of course.

Price points will vary, and you can get some well-made boots for around $300. The really great stuff will often be twice that much. Middleton says, “the best thing to do is wait and save up until you can buy the version you actually want because its impossible to cut corners and still deliver the product you want.” So if you can be patient, save up. If you can’t, try buying secondhand. But if you simply can’t wait, you’ll do well enough with plenty of the mid-tier brands until you’re ready to upgrade.

Style is subjective, we know—that’s the fun of it. But we’re serious about helping our audience get dressed. Whether it’s the best white sneakers, the flyest affordable suits, or the need-to-know menswear drops of the week, GQ Recommends’ perspective is built on years of hands-on experience, an insider awareness of what’s in and what’s next, and a mission to find the best version of everything out there, at every price point.

Our staffers aren’t able to try on every single piece of clothing you read about on GQ.com (fashion moves fast these days), but we have an intimate knowledge of each brand’s strengths and know the hallmarks of quality clothing—from materials and sourcing, to craftsmanship, to sustainability efforts that aren’t just greenwashing. GQ Recommends heavily emphasizes our own editorial experience with those brands, how they make their clothes, and how those clothes have been reviewed by customers. Bottom line: GQ wouldn’t tell you to wear it if we wouldn’t.

We make every effort to cast as wide of a net as possible, with an eye on identifying the best options across three key categories: quality, fit, and price.

To kick off the process, we enlist the GQ Recommends braintrust to vote on our contenders. Some of the folks involved have worked in retail, slinging clothes to the masses; others have toiled for small-batch menswear labels; all spend way too much time thinking about what hangs in their closets.

We lean on that collective experience to guide our search, culling a mix of household names, indie favorites, and the artisanal imprints on the bleeding-edge of the genre. Then we narrow down the assortment to the picks that scored the highest across quality, fit, and price.

Across the majority of our buying guides, our team boasts firsthand experience with the bulk of our selects, but a handful are totally new to us. So after several months of intense debate, we tally the votes, collate the anecdotal evidence, and emerge with a list of what we believe to be the absolute best of the category right now, from the tried-and-true stalwarts to the modern disruptors, the affordable beaters to the wildly expensive (but wildly worth-it) designer riffs.

Whatever your preferences, whatever your style, there's bound to be a superlative version on this list for you.

#6707. 18 Best Raincoats for Men 2026, According to GQ Editors

Decades ago, your only options for rainy weather coverage were some kind of trench coat, a coarse wool overcoat, or, if you really needed to be out in it, a thick rubber sack that kept you dry but didn’t breathe. Thankfully, material science has advanced.

There are, of course, umbrellas? And we won’t stop you looking into those. But ask anyone from the Pacific Northwest, and they’ll tell you their rain jacket does all that work with ease. That’s because raincoats aren’t an accessory to your outfit: they’re the main focus. A good raincoat can turn your sad ensemble into head-turning Paparazzi fodder, or at the very least, keep you dry and stylish when it’s wet out.

Still not convinced? We figured—so we tested the best rain jackets and coats for men on the market right now, from the tried-and-true natural-fabric mainstays to the buzzy, newfangled riffs, and everything in between. Here they are.

Arc'teryx's Beta LT jacket is the pinnacle of no-BS rain defense, beloved by wannabe Everest climbers, GQ Recommends editors, and meme-savvy TikTokers alike. Its custom Gore-Tex fabrication will keep you from getting soaked during the harshest of downpours, while its breathable pores work overtime to let water vapor (a.k.a. sweat) escape. That sleek, streamlined silhouette also lends itself naturally to a range of pairings, from straight-fit jeans and sneakers to a full-on “Scaling Kilimanjaro” kit—cargos, backpack, hiking boots—perfect for all of your wildcard outdoor activities.

It’s an investment, but hundreds of guys were showering in theirs for a reason: If you need top-tier weather protection and long-term durability—and are willing to invest—this is still the best rain jacket for serious outdoor use. That said, most dudes chasing the clout that Arc’teryx affords won’t need to max out its capabilities. Still, it’s good to know that if you’re ever caught in a downpour that won’t let up, the Beta’s the one waterproof jacket that won’t leave you high and, uh, wet.

When it comes to outdoor gear that keeps up with Mother Nature, Patagonia’s among the first to come to mind. The OG gorpcore label has more than its fair share of bangers for every climate from the iconic Baggies shorts to its range of plush fleece jackets, and when it comes to rainy weather, there’s the Torrentshell jacket. It’s a canonical piece of kit for Patagonia and has kept customers dry and fly for years.

The latest version features a three-layer shell system that maximizes water-repellency and breathability with a 100% recycled nylon face fabric. It features toasty microfleece-lined handwarmer pockets, welted zippers for water protection (plus pit zips for ventilation), and the entire thing can pack down into itself. So pack it up, throw it in your bag, and bust it out whenever the weather turns out to be worse than what the weatherman says. With a feature set like that, you’d expect to pay through the nose, but we’re thrilled to say that it doesn’t even break $200.

The advancements in rain-wicking technology tickles our lizard brains as much as watching a rock-crushing machine turn a boulder into pebbles. But as much as we appreciate multi-layered DWR-coated materials and heat-sealed seams give us joy, we also have a great reverence and appreciation for rain coats of yore. Enter the waxed jacket, a time-tested fabric used by sailors off the British coast since the 1800s.

If you’re gonna get a waxed jacket, you’d best get a Barbour, the reigning champ of waxed jackets. Barbour has more options and collaborations than ever, so it can be tricky to get your head around what’s what but for our money, the classic Beaufort is the Goldilocks of wax jackets. Where the Bedale is shorter and styles like the Border run down to the mid-thigh, the Beaufort is just right but still long enough to layer over your sport coats. Whichever you choose, don’t forget to re-wax at home after a few seasons. Guys seem to be nervous to do it, but it’s easy, and everytime I’ve re-waxed mine, people compliment me on how great it looks. The reward for your grunt work is a jacket you'll keep for decades to come.

This season, J.Press has unearthed a Rare Barbour: the Burghley. It hasn’t been in the brand’s regular rotation for a number of years, earning it a following among collectors in places like Japan. But now it’s back in all its sweeping, full-length glory.

Originally designed to extend past the knees (and over the saddle and tops of riding boots) the Burghley protects the rider from rain and thorns. It’s a dramatic silhouette that will set you apart from all the cropped Transport and Bedales out there. Sure, it has equestrian heritage, but you needn’t be keeping a stable to wear one. It’s a great alternative to a trench, at home in the city as well as the country. And yes, it’s still made in England.

The perfect trench coat does exist, and Burberry, inventor of the style's signature gabardine fabric in 1879, cracked the code on it long ago. In the nearly 150 years since, surprisingly little has changed about the silhouette, and for good reason. The classic fit means it's roomy enough to layer over your slouchiest Armani-inspired suiting, while the belted design gives it instant sophistication. Plus, the lining and under-collar check pattern is unmistakably Burberry—an investment-worthy flex since the coat was invented.

Its WWI heritage is well-documented—British officers wore it in the trenches, using details like the shoulder flap for rifle recoil and belt rings for canteens and grenades serving real military functions. Today, it’s less about battlefield utility and more about how that water-resistant cotton gabardine will keep you dry in light city rain. I like the black, but the classic tan is always an option for you too.

Another entry from Vancouver—what can we say, they know a few things about rain up there. Indie label James Coward sources fabric from mills known for their quality and considered production practices, then makes its garments locally in its workshop. This coat is cut from the brand’s durable, waterproof ‘Mastershield cloth, a dense weave that offers protection from the elements. A generous silhouette and roomy raglan sleeves give it natural gravitas in gloomy weather, all while keeping the look relaxed and easy to layer.

Whereas the Arc’teryx Beta is the brand’s versatile all-mountain shell, the Alpha SV is built for technical climbing and severe weather. A helmet-compatible hood, harness-ready chest pockets and pit zips make it the top dog of storm shells.

HAVEN puts a GORPcore spin on the refined, city-ready trench made from three-layer GORE-TEX PRO, a more rugged fabric typically found in shells for climbing and backcountry skiing. It’s serious storm gear with a city sensibility, ready to make you feel invincible during a rainy commute.

The wrap coat is Lemaire’s runaway hit. But this Ventile-cotton style is a rainy-day flex: densely woven, breathable cotton that swells when wet to help keep wind and rain out, without synthetic fibres or coatings. Chic.

Menswear can get a little obsessive about fabric specs and provenance. But when a jacket is this cool, you get why. 100 percent Giza cotton, woven at maximum density in Saitama, Japan and originally developed for the British Air Force and later recommissioned by Japan’s Coast Guard. Yeah, that’s the good stuff.

Prep never left, but J.Press is certainly on its way back. The label’s updated take on a classic hooded parka keeps the familiar military shape sans PFAS with natural Ventile cotton. Choose classic army green, or eye-popping orange.

Haider Ackermann's take on Canada Goose is coming into focus. The brand’s Snow Goose sub-label is more Milan than Manitoba, trading logo-heavy designs for cool colours and modern silhouettes that go well beyond down parkas.

Making a good rain jacket might not take a degree in nuclear science, but Outdoor Research’s founder was actually a nuclear physicist turned avid mountaineer. This stripped-back shell sheds weight and packs small without skimping on protection. Stash it and forget it.

Marmot, meanwhile didn’t pull back on features to shave weight. This soft, quiet waterproof shell is built for movement, with an “Angel-Wing” design that lets you reach overhead without the hem riding up and putting your backside on display.

Reigning Champ has also gone well beyond sweats and tees. This workhorse of a rain jacket is more downtown than summit in style but still meets the moment with GORE-TEX construction and a very digestible price.

Italian fabric. Fifth-generation Portuguese construction. New York City design. That’s the mix behind Knickerbocker’s full-length trench. Its tightly woven, water-repellent fabric helps keep rain out all with the kind of easy, downtown cool Knickerbocker does so well.

Uniqlo’s even more affordable sister brand, GU, delivers a lot of jacket for the money The throwback piping and punchy colours nod to the ’90s, while the water- and wind-resistant fabric makes it practical, too.

A waxed Harrington probably isn’t for a downpour unless your trousers are waxed, too, but it’s ideal for breezy, drizzly, and sunny fall days alike. And, aye, the waxed fabric comes straight from Scotland. No messing about.

Who wants to drag a sopping wet umbrella around only to forget it at the restaurant or in the back of a cab? When you’ve got a trench coat with your cell and headphones in the pockets, it’s almost impossible to lose. Through light mists, heavy rain, and even torrential downpours, the best rain coats are, ironically, the drippiest too. They’ll prevent you from showing up to the function like a poor wet dog and instead make your arrival a warm, dry welcome.

Trench coats that aren’t the only coats vying to shield you from the rain, though. Gorpcore’s popularity has given us waves of tech-y jackets, parkas, and anoraks all wrought from futuristic multi-layered materials that balance breathability with waterproof ability. The result is a cementing of this 21st century sportswear into must-have menswear. And let’s not forget the workwear-adjacent waxed jackets born from sailing the Scottish seas in 15th-century Scotland. With their rugged good looks and a lust for patina, they’ve become stylish staples when the weather’s all doom and gloom.

Style is subjective, we know—that’s the fun of it. But we’re serious about helping our audience get dressed. Whether it’s the best white sneakers, the flyest affordable suits, or the need-to-know menswear drops of the week, GQ Recommends’ perspective is built on years of hands-on experience, an insider awareness of what’s in and what’s next, and a mission to find the best version of everything out there, at every price point.

Our staffers aren’t able to try on every single piece of clothing you read about on GQ.com (fashion moves fast these days), but we have an intimate knowledge of each brand’s strengths and know the hallmarks of quality clothing—from materials and sourcing, to craftsmanship, to sustainability efforts that aren’t just greenwashing. GQ Recommends heavily emphasizes our own editorial experience with those brands, how they make their clothes, and how those clothes have been reviewed by customers. Bottom line: GQ wouldn’t tell you to wear it if we wouldn’t.

We make every effort to cast as wide of a net as possible, with an eye on identifying the best options across three key categories: quality, fit, and price.

To kick off the process, we enlist the GQ Recommends braintrust to vote on our contenders. Some of the folks involved have worked in retail, slinging clothes to the masses; others have toiled for small-batch menswear labels; all spend way too much time thinking about what hangs in their closets.

We lean on that collective experience to guide our search, culling a mix of household names, indie favorites, and the artisanal imprints on the bleeding-edge of the genre. Then we narrow down the assortment to the picks that scored the highest across quality, fit, and price.

Across the majority of our buying guides, our team boasts firsthand experience with the bulk of our selects, but a handful are totally new to us. So after several months of intense debate, we tally the votes, collate the anecdotal evidence, and emerge with a list of what we believe to be the absolute best of the category right now, from the tried-and-true stalwarts to the modern disruptors, the affordable beaters to the wildly expensive (but wildly worth-it) designer riffs.

Whatever your preferences, whatever your style, there's bound to be a superlative version on this list for you. (Read more about GQ's testing process here.)

#6706. Designers Say These Are the Best Colors to Pair With Blue in Any Room

They perfectly complement the rainbow's most versatile hue.

A designer-favorite color to use in the home is beautiful, calming blue. Evoking natural elements like the sea and the sky, this serene hue can make any room feel more relaxed and welcoming, yet also more vibrant and bold, depending on the shades. And if this versatile hue is one you’re thinking about adding to your home, check out these beautiful colors that go with blue, proven by these designer spaces.

No matter your design style, this list of 38 colors that go with blue serves as evidence that the hue is as versatile as a neutral and can elevate any space you use it in. Read on to find your new favorite color scheme here.

To contrast the fanciful chinoiserie wallpaper in this San Francisco apartment, interior designer Caitlin Jones Ghajar and her team went bold and chose a warm yellow for the velvet bed frame. “This particular kind of blue lends itself really well to the vistas that are beyond, so you get some Bay view looks,” she says.

In this Amber Lewis–designed kitchen, the duck egg blue cabinetry is grounded by the rust red and cream floor tile. The hue, Light Blue by Farrow & Ball, can lean almost gray in soft lighting, making it a versatile neutral ready to pair with warm tones.

The deep, black accents in this kitchen add drama and depth to the slate blue cabinetry. The graphic color pairing is modern and masculine, while not trying to compete with the lush nature outdoors. Melissa Anderson of OAD Interiors designed this immersive space in Woodstock, New York.

Blue and yellow is a classic and cheerful color pairing. Elevate the duo by choosing a hue with a slightly greener tone, like a chartreuse. In this guest room by French & French, the cool light blue prevents a too-neon appearance.

Pairing a cool tone with a warm tone is a failsafe approach, and this bedroom by Serena Dugan does it expertly. A golden tan wallpaper softens the vibrant indigo accents on the headboard and window treatments. If you’re feeling bold, throw in some orange for added impact.

When you're unsure of what colors go with blue the best, simply opt for more of the same, as French & French did here. Perfect pattern play is achieved in this lounge through the matching blue and white palettes. A cobalt blue wall ties it all together.

For an impactful palette, add some glitz to your shade of blue. This living room, designed by Suzanne Kasler, utilizes metallics like brass and bronze to elevate the everyday hue to glamorous new heights.

In this Santa Fe home designed by the duo behind French & French, a rich, terra-cotta red balances the vibrant blue tones. To ground the swirling marbled wallpaper on the ceiling, the grand bookcases and coordinating rug (and sneaky chair piping!) incorporate the hue throughout the entire space.

In House Beautiful’s 2024 Whole Home, the guest room is cocooned in a mustard and navy color palette. Blue and yellow primary colors play off one another for a vibrant interior. Use neutral bedding to ground the bright shades.

Pink and blue can often feel like a baby shower or gender reveal, but take inspiration from designer Dane Austin to know how to use them correctly. By combining a pastel with a more vibrant tone, you can elevate the color pairing from juvenile to sophisticated.

Look to nature for hues to use with blue. This chic powder room, complete with a navy blue sink, is swathed in a floral green wallpaper. This color pairing is cheerful and can brighten up a smaller space, especially this one by Liz Carroll, which lacks windows.

Adorned in shades of cobalt and navy, the primary bedroom in our 2024 Whole Home in Asheville, North Carolina, continues the moody color palette with an ochre wallpaper. The hues are simultaneously subdued yet impactful, ideal for a serene night’s sleep.

A spa-like and timeless blue and gray color palette can brighten even the dingiest basements. To ensure nothing looks out of place, keep both hues at the same vibrancy level (e.g., a light blue and a light gray).

To add drama to this teal bedroom, designer Lisa Tharp opted for a totally red upholstered bed frame, and somehow it works. Blue and red are both primary colors, which means they pair well when one starts to lean toward the other’s complement (like the slightly green-toned blue walls).

In this playful kids' bathroom, a bright blue is the focal point color in the space. Looking to the color wheel for a clue, homeowner Ashley Maddox and designer Hilary Walker opted for a burnt orange shower curtain as the accent hue.

Pairing a myriad of cool-toned colors can create a sophisticated palette when done well, like in this Boston apartment. A purple rug complements the blue sectional sofa because they have similar textiles, tones, and brightness levels.

If you’re a fan of the blue and yellow primary pair, you’ll love this elevated option. Pairing a deep, rich blue, like this navy kitchen, with a metallic brass accent is a great way to add some glamour and texture to your space.

Monochrome color palettes can be really impactful when done with various shades of the same hue. Take this living room by Mark D. Sikes for example. The deep blue upholstery on the sofa and ottoman ground the lighter blue used to color drench the walls and ceiling.

Avery Cox of Avery Cox Design used a tonal blue for the wall to give this room an immersive effect and paired it with pops of bubblegum pink, purple, and citron yellow. The colors are the perfect pick-me-up when you're lounging indoors on a rainy day.

This robin's egg blue wallpaper is speckled with ivory and forest green to make daydreaming easy. The ivory mirror and green accessories designer Shannon Eddings added to this dining room are the supporting characters that soften the blue so it doesn't overwhelm the space.

#6705. Legal Shrooms Are Coming

Could psilocybin offer a breakthrough for treatment-resistant depression?

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

As part of the Future of Beauty Awards, the ELLE beauty team looked ahead at what will be next in beauty and wellness.

Shrooms are passed around at parties as a hangover-free alternative to alcohol. Zoë Kravitz, playing a fictionalized version of herself, accidentally overdosed on them in an episode of Apple TV+’s The Studio. But soon, they may be officially recognized as more than a way to let loose and could be used as a powerful weapon to combat serious depression, with the potential of being legalized in the U.S. by early 2027. Psilocybin is currently legal in some parts of Oregon and Colorado for treating mental health conditions in certain settings; at the federal level, it’s classified as a Schedule I controlled substance, considered to have no medical use and a high potential for abuse. But with biotechnology company Compass Pathways having achieved two consecutively positive Phase 3 trials for its COMP360 psilocybin therapy in treatment-resistant depression, that may change soon. Shrooms could make history as the first classic psychedelic drug (working on a particular serotonin receptor in the brain) approved by the FDA.

In an unaffiliated 2026 study in the journal Nature Medicine, scientists found that people with treatment-resistant depression who were given COMP360’s formulation of psilocybin had lower depression scores than those treated with a placebo. A Johns Hopkins Medicine study published in the Journal of Psychopharmacology in 2022 showed that the antidepressant effects of psilocybin therapy, alongside supportive psychotherapy, may last for a full year. It’s already legal for therapeutic treatment of some mental health conditions in countries like Australia, New Zealand, and Switzerland. Jay Godfrey, co-founder and CEO of Nushama, which is billed as the world’s largest psychedelic wellness center in New York City, uses a familiar psychological construct to speculate about how it might work. “There is a theory that psilocybin causes a quieting of an area of our brain called the default mode network, where your ego is housed,” he says. “Your own righteousness, search for validation, opinions, judgments—all those things are housed in ego. Psilocybin creates what we call ego dissolution: You get to observe yourself in the absence of your judgment, opinions, and all the walls that are around you that keep you safe. It allows you to see your traumas and your life story through a much more loving and compassionate [lens].”

Traditional depression medication, like selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors (SSRIs), is typically taken every day to balance serotonin levels in the brain. Psilocybin would likely be different. “You’re taking one to four doses per year,” says Myriam Barthes, the co-founder and CEO of Journey Clinical, the largest psychedelic-assisted psychotherapy platform in the United States. “The experience itself will last about five to seven hours.” Godfrey says that a standard dose of magic mushrooms could likely be 25 milligrams, and both Godfrey and Barthes stress that, unlike legalized marijuana, psilocybin may be regulated as a pharmaceutical drug, with each experience taking place in a clinic under the watchful eye of a medical professional trained in psilocybin therapy. It would be very unlikely that you could have the option to get a prescription for shrooms and take them at home, according to Lief Fenno, MD, PhD, chair of the American Psychiatric Association Council on Addiction Psychiatry. “Patient safety is paramount, so these are highly controlled situations with trained professionals,” Fenno says. “That’s very different than you simply taking a bunch of mushrooms or dropping acid.”

According to the Journal of Clinical Psychiatry, around 2.8 million people have treatment-resistant depression, medically defined by the FDA as having tried two different antidepressants and seen limited improvement (though there’s no prevailing consensus on this definition). Christopher Pittenger, MD, PhD, the Elizabeth Mears and House Jameson professor of psychiatry at Yale School of Medicine, says that there are a few compelling theories for how psilocybin could help them. “One is that it increases brain plasticity,” he says. A healthy brain is more clay than glass, able to be shaped by experience. When someone is severely depressed, their brain is often less able to make new connections and change; psilocybin might be able to make it more pliable. A second theory is that shrooms can alter brain circuits, which control how your mind processes information. One of those circuits is the default mode network that Godfrey mentioned—and Pittenger says that when this network is impacted, you may be able to view yourself differently, which can positively impact your mental health.

Because psilocybin would likely only be available in a clinic under the supervision of medically trained professionals if it gets approved, Pittenger says, there’s little risk of it being abused. “People can use and overuse it, but I don’t think that the medical approval is going to increase the ability to use it recreationally,” he says. The main worry, he adds, is that psilocybin could cause psychosis in people who are already at risk for it, like those with a medical or family history of schizophrenia or bipolar disorder. Headaches, nausea, anxiety, dizziness, and elevated blood pressure have been associated with its administration in clinical trials. The drug may also slightly increase blood pressure, so people with high blood pressure should likely avoid it. A full medical and psychiatric evaluation that covers medical history and past psychedelic use should be done prior to this treatment.

One persistent problem with the trials for psilocybin therapy is that it’s uniquely hard to blind them, which means that patients usually know if they’re being given a psychedelic drug. “Patients in the trials know whether or not they’re getting a compound that creates an altered state of consciousness, or if they’re getting a neutral placebo,” Fenno says. “There are trials ongoing that are trying to understand how much of the very real therapeutic effect we see is due specifically to the psilocybin.”

A recent RAND survey found that public support for legalized psilocybin (currently 23 percent) nearly matches the support for cannabis in the mid-’90s, and Colorado and Oregon have already decriminalized it. But their experience raises caution. “We showed that with decriminalization, the number of adults using psilocybin about doubled in each state,” says Joshua C. Black, PhD, a statistical scientist who recently conducted an analysis of the two states for the American Medical Association. “The size and timing of that increase suggested that many people are using outside of the legal frameworks in both states, at least in the initial years.” The study notes that people in decriminalized states may have felt more comfortable self-reporting psilocybin use, since there are fewer legal repercussions. While this increase might sound alarming, Black says that prescription access will be much more tightly controlled than access under broad decriminalization. “FDA-approved drugs will have substantial controls over manufacturing and distribution, and I don’t think that would generate the same pattern.”

Whether or not psilocybin gets FDA approval, the fact that we’re talking about a different kind of depression treatment makes some experts optimistic. “One of the most exciting parts about all of this research is that it’s got the entire country talking about mental illness, and new treatments for it,” Fenno says. So while shroom chocolates surreptitiously handed out at a dinner party will likely remain illegal and you probably won’t be able to buy them at a dispensary, people with treatment-resistant depression may soon have a new option available under medical supervision.

Editor’s note: If you or someone you know is experiencing a mental health crisis or depression, dial 988 or (800) 662-HELP for emergency help or consult the resources available on the American Psychiatric Association’s website (psychiatry.org).

#6703. How 12 Famous Watches Got Their Nicknames

From the 'Steve McQueen' Rolex to the 'Holy Grail' Omega.

A few weeks ago, Casio unveiled a new range of fully analogue G-Shock watches, billed as its biggest launch in decades. But what immediately caught collectors' imaginations wasn't the technology inside the new 2500 Series. It was the shape of the cases, whose rounded rectangular outlines bear more than a passing resemblance to the Patek Philippe Nautilus.

There’s precedent here. When Casio’s eight-sided GA-2100 appeared in 2019, enthusiasts immediately clocked its likeness to Audemars Piguet's Royal Oak and christened it the 'CasiOak'. The nickname stuck and has since become shorthand for the entire family. (Perhaps this is what happens when you launch watches with names like GA-2100.)

So what to call the new Nautilus lookalike? Suggestions have included “Nauty-Less” and “CasiNau,” but “CasioNaut” seems to have won out.

Elsewhere in recent watch news, Seiko has been having fun with another nickname. Most watch fans will be familiar with the term “panda,” used to describe a chronograph with a white main dial and two black sub-dials, an arrangement said to resemble the face of the bamboo-munching bear. Seiko's new Power Design Project Panda takes the resemblance to its logical conclusion, with actual panda eyes, ears and, even a little wagging tail on the case.

And next week, Piaget will release a new version of its Andy Warhol watch, a name collectors had been using for decades before the brand finally made it official in 2024, following an agreement with the Andy Warhol Foundation.

Clearly, the peculiar business of giving watches nicknames is alive and well. Which gives us an excuse, as if one were needed, to run through a dozen great watch nicknames and the stories behind them.

Some are named after astronauts, actors, and models. Others have more unusual stories, involving a collaboration that never happened, a celebrity who almost certainly never wore the watch named after him, and an American president who wasn't even allowed to accept his watch.

Piaget ref. 15102 and its modern descendants

In the 1970s, Piaget was making some of the most extravagant watches money could buy, with chunky gold cases, colorful stone dials, and an impressive list of celebrity customers. Among them was Andy Warhol, who owned at least seven Piagets, including a large, cushion-shaped gold model, reference 15102, which he bought in 1973. (Four of his watches are now in Piaget’s collection.)

Six years later, Warhol met Yves Piaget, the founder’s great-grandson, and the two became friends. Warhol joined the Piaget Society, the glamorous circle of artists, designers, and celebrities cultivated by the company.

Introduced in 1972, the 15102 was produced for just five years before disappearing from Piaget’s catalogue. The design returned in 2014 under the name Black Tie, although collectors had long since nicknamed the original the “Andy Warhol.” Ten years later, the company finally made the name official, signing a licensing agreement with the Andy Warhol Foundation for the Visual Arts in 2024.

Piaget has since developed a collection bearing the artist’s name, with designs that draw on his taste for unconventional colors and materials. Among the first was a Clou de Paris edition featuring a blue meteorite dial surrounded by rows of tiny gold pyramids.

Warhol’s association has done vintage prices no harm, either. In May this year, an example resembling his own sold at Sotheby’s in Geneva for CHF166,399 – about $200,000.

Omega Speedmaster ref. 311.30.42.30.01.004

The Omega Speedmaster “Tintin” takes its name from a collaboration with the Belgian cartoon character that never quite happened.

In 2013, Omega released a version of its Speedmaster Professional chronograph (officially called the Racing) with an unusual red-and-white checkered pattern around the edge of its black dial. It was inspired by the rocket in Hergé’s Tintin adventures Destination Moon and Explorers on the Moon.

The original plan was to produce the watch in partnership with the owners of the Tintin rights. Early prototypes even featured the rocket on the dial, with a special case back to match. But the deal fell through, and Omega went ahead without the Tintin branding, leaving a rather curious design detail whose inspiration the company couldn't openly celebrate.

The story came to light when Robert-Jan Broer, founder of the watch website Fratello, spoke to Omega’s head of product development. Once collectors understood the connection, the “Tintin” nickname stuck. Illustrations from the comics accompanying Fratello’s coverage were subsequently removed at the request of the rights holder.

That said, the owners weren't opposed to putting the character on a watch altogether. Omega’s Swatch Group stablemate Swatch had already produced two official Tintin models for the character’s 75th anniversary in 2004, including a Happy Birthday Tintin edition limited to 9,999 pieces. Omega simply never managed to get its own collaboration over the line.

Seiko 6139, particularly the yellow-dial 6139-6005

Omega may have the Moonwatch, but Seiko can lay claim to the first automatic chronograph worn in space. And unlike the Omegas issued by Nasa, the Seiko in question was bought by the astronaut himself.

In June 1972, US Air Force pilot William Pogue paid $71 for a yellow-dial Seiko 6139 chronograph. He was training for NASA’s Skylab program and needed a watch to time engine burns. Like other astronauts, Pogue was due to receive an Omega Speedmaster, the chronograph Nasa had qualified for spaceflight, but his hadn't yet been delivered.

By the time his Omega arrived, however, Pogue had become so attached to the Seiko that he decided to take it into space as well.

When Skylab 4 launched in November 1973, the Seiko travelled in a pocket of Pogue’s spacesuit. During the mission’s 84 days in orbit, he wore it on his left wrist, with the Omega on his right. (The Seiko remained set to US Central Time.)

For years, the Seiko’s part in space history went largely unknown. Then American watch collector David Bruno spotted a reference to it in a mission transcript and contacted Pogue to find out what he'd worn. In 2008, the astronaut consigned the watch through the Astronaut Scholarship Foundation. It sold at Heritage Auctions for a surprisingly modest $5,975, complete with its original receipt and Pogue’s handwritten account.

The original Seiko 6139 has long been discontinued, but yellow-dial examples are now widely known as “Pogues.” They're still relatively affordable, too. Seiko has since revisited the look with the solar-powered Prospex SSC947, but it's an interpretation rather than a direct reissue.

There is one problem with the Rolex Explorer II colloquially known as the “Steve McQueen.” Nobody has been able to establish that Steve McQueen ever wore one.

His Rolex of choice was a Submariner. Yet the Explorer II association has become so firmly established that even Sotheby’s used the nickname when offering an example in May 2022, while the description below acknowledged that no convincing evidence existed for the connection. (Who first decided to attach the actor’s name to the watch remains a mystery.)

In fact, the same model has another nickname with an easier-to-fathom explanation. Freccione is Italian for “big arrow,” referring to the whopping orange 24-hour hand that dominates its dial.

Introduced in 1971, the Explorer II was designed for cave explorers and others who might spend extended periods without natural daylight. Its extra hand completed one revolution every 24 hours, allowing the wearer to distinguish day from night.

With its fixed steel bezel, busy dial, and that unmistakable orange hand, the original Explorer II looks quite unlike most of Rolex’s better-known models. It has since become a firm favorite with collectors.

In 1987, Omega introduced a Speedmaster that put a new spin on the familiar Moonwatch. The ref. 376.0822 featured an automatic movement, a day and date display, a 24-hour indicator, and an additional hand to count elapsed minutes. It made for a busy, rather technical-looking watch, and one that wasn't destined for a long production run.

Its relative scarcity would eventually catch the attention of American collector and Omega expert Chuck Maddox.

In 1999, Maddox was offered a 376.0822 in Germany for around $600. At roughly the same time, another Speedmaster appeared for sale in Iowa at a similar price. Faced with a choice, he went for the Iowa watch, reasoning that dealing with a seller he could visit in person was less risky than buying from overseas.

Almost immediately, he regretted passing up the German example. Finding another proved far more difficult than he had expected. A watch appeared in Japan with a price tag of more than $10,000. Another opportunity slipped away at auction. Eventually, with help from a fellow enthusiast, he managed to track one down.

Maddox later wrote about his hunt on his website and in online watch forums, comparing his obsession with finding the elusive Speedmaster to King Arthur’s search for the Holy Grail. (Watch collectors have never knowingly undersold a story.) He began referring to the watch simply as “the Grail,” and the nickname caught on among fellow collectors.

It's a tale that will be painfully familiar to anyone who's ever collected watches: the one that got away, and the lengths you'll go to for a second chance to spend a load of money.

When quartz technology arrived in the late 1960s, its promise was greater accuracy and, eventually, affordable watches for almost everyone. Rolex, naturally, took a different path.

Its first quartz watch, launched in 1970, was available exclusively in gold. The white-gold version of the ref. 5100 cost 18,300 Swiss francs before tax. According to Rolex expert James Dowling, that was roughly the price of a Porsche 912 and two-and-a-half times what the company charged for a Day-Date on its President bracelet.

Inside was the Beta 21, an early Swiss quartz movement developed jointly by a consortium of manufacturers. The technology was remarkable enough, but the watch looked like no Rolex that had come before it. Its large, angular case, broad bezel and integrated gold bracelet were a world away from the familiar designs of the period.

Italian collectors took to calling it the “Texano,” or “Texan,” presumably in recognition of its considerable size and extravagance.

Only around 1,000 numbered examples are thought to have been made, mostly in yellow gold. More than half a century later, the “Texano” remains as rare and extravagant as ever.

Nothing to do with glamorous magazine models, as it happens. The nickname comes from a book published in 2009 by Zenith historian and collector Manfred Rössler, who chose a photograph of the El Primero A3818 for the cover of Zenith: Swiss Watch Manufacture Since 1865. His reason for picking that particular watch? He simply liked blue dials. Fair enough.

Introduced in 1971, the A3818 was a 37mm steel chronograph with an angular case housing Zenith's celebrated El Primero movement. Its vertically brushed blue dial featured contrasting sub-dials and distinctive shark's-tooth markings around the edge.

Only 1,000 examples were made, according to Zenith, and the model had long since disappeared from production when Rössler's book brought it back to collectors' attention. Zenith revived the design in 2020, with a lightweight titanium Airweight edition following in 2022. Both had “Cover Girl” as part of their official names.

It doesn't get much more ’60s than Finnish fashion model Nina Rindt, married to Austrian Formula One driver Jochen Rindt and a regular fixture in the paddock during the sport's glamorous heyday. Photographs from the late 1960s show her watching races from the pit lane, often wearing a white-dial Universal Genève Compax chronograph on a wide leather cuff. The combination of glamorous wearer, racing connection, and period photographs proved irresistible to watch collectors, who began calling the model the “Nina Rindt.” Its black-dial counterpart acquired the even better moniker “Evil Nina.”

The nicknames helped transform the watch's fortunes. In the early 2010s, as collectors became increasingly interested in vintage chronographs with famous associations, prices for the “Nina Rindt” rose dramatically. As Eric Wind, founder of Wind Vintage and a former Christie's watch specialist, recalled: “That watch went from $3,000 ordinarily and was worth $40,000 within a year.” One particularly fine example sold at Christie's in 2015 for $47,500.

When Universal Genève relaunched in April this year, the Compax was central to its comeback, with new chronographs inspired by both versions. The watches now have automatic movements but retain the contrasting sub-dials of the originals—and are even offered on the wide leather cuff that helped make Nina's watch so recognizable.

In the 1983 film Octopussy, Roger Moore's James Bond wore a Seiko G757 Sports 100 that had been given a special upgrade by Q Branch. Its small circular digital display functioned as a tracking screen, allowing Bond to follow a transmitter concealed inside a Fabergé egg being sought by the film's villain, Kamal Khan.

Almost 30 years later, in 2012, Casio introduced the AE-1200, an inexpensive digital world timer with a striking resemblance to Bond's gadget. It had a similarly angular case, multiple digital displays and, most importantly, a little circular clock that seemed to recall the tracking screen on Moore's Seiko.

Enthusiasts spotted the resemblance and came up with a pun on Casino Royale, the 2006 Bond film starring Daniel Craig. Thus the “Casio Royale” was born, a nickname involving a different watch, a different manufacturer, and an entirely different Bond film.

The AE-1200 remains in Casio's catalog today, costing $55 and looking much as it did in 2012. Its miniature world map indicates the selected time zone, while the little circular display shows the time. It also has five alarms, a stopwatch, and a countdown timer. The Fabergé egg tracker is not included.

By 1972, Bulova was advertising the water resistance of its Oceanographer dive watch with the words “666 FEET” printed prominently on the dial. That's approximately 203 metres, although quite why the company chose such an oddly specific figure rather than a round 200 meters isn't entirely clear.

Given the number's rather ominous biblical associations, it didn't take long for enthusiasts to christen the watch the alliteratively pleasing “Devil Diver.”

The Oceanographer had other things going for it, too. The original was a handsome example of early-1970s dive-watch design, with a substantial cushion-shaped case, a rotating bezel, and a dial with extravagantly bold markers intended to be easily read underwater.

In 2017, Bulova teamed up with New York vintage-watch dealer Analog/Shift to gauge interest in reviving one of its archive designs. The Oceanographer came out on top. A memorable nickname with a whiff of danger presumably didn't hurt.

The watch duly returned in 2018, including an orange-dial edition limited to, naturally, 666 pieces. Since then, Bulova has produced smaller versions and an Oceanographer GMT capable of tracking a second time zone.

More than 50 years later, Bulova still prints “666 FEET” on the dial, although its official specifications now give the water resistance as a rather neater 200 meters.

Audemars Piguet Royal Oak Offshore ref. 25721

“You have spoilt my watch, you're a murderer!”

According to designer Emmanuel Gueit, this was Gérald Genta's reaction when he first saw the Royal Oak Offshore, a considerably beefier version of the Royal Oak he had designed for Audemars Piguet in 1972.

AP had been hoping to recapture some of the impact of the original Royal Oak, which had caused a sensation with its unusually large steel case, integrated bracelet, and extravagant price. By the early 1990s, the company wanted something to appeal to a younger audience, and Gueit was given the job.

His answer, launched in 1993, was the Royal Oak Offshore. The original's 39mm case grew to 42mm, enormous by the standards of the day. It was thicker, too, with a chronograph movement, rubber-coated crown and pushers, and a conspicuous gasket visible beneath the bezel. The Offshore name came from powerboat racing, a suitably muscular association for such an imposing watch.

The industry quickly christened it “The Beast.” Genta himself reportedly compared it to a sea elephant, which, frankly, would have made an even better nickname.

Even within Audemars Piguet, enthusiasm was mixed. The company's archive records that some insiders hoped the project would fail, while the first hundred examples had only Royal Oak engraved on their case backs, without the Offshore name. Hardly a ringing vote of confidence.

Customers eventually came around, helped by celebrity associations, particularly with Arnold Schwarzenegger. The Offshore went on to help establish the fashion for oversized luxury watches, which must have had Genta sobbing even harder into his fondue.

Omega Speedmaster Apollo XI ref. BA145.022-69

When Neil Armstrong and Buzz Aldrin landed on the moon in July 1969, Omega had good reason to celebrate. Its Speedmaster chronographs had accompanied NASA astronauts throughout the Apollo program, and the company decided to mark the achievement by making 1,014 special gold editions.

These were considerably more luxurious than the steel watches issued to the astronauts, with 18-carat yellow-gold cases, bracelets, and dials, plus burgundy bezels. The first two were intended for President Richard Nixon and Vice President Spiro Agnew, complete with individual engravings.

There was just one problem. American rules governing gifts to public officials meant neither man could accept an expensive gold watch from a Swiss manufacturer. Both were returned to Omega, where they remain in the company's museum in Switzerland today.

Other recipients were luckier. Astronauts received their watches at a gala dinner in Houston in November 1969, while most of the remaining production was offered for public sale. Collectors subsequently nicknamed the reference the “Nixon,” after the president who never got to wear his.

The watches have since become highly collectible, particularly those with a connection to the Apollo astronauts. An example sold at Bonhams in 2024 for HK$384,000 [just under $50K], while Neil Armstrong's own gold Speedmaster, number 17, fetched $2.125 million in April last year.

Of course, Omega couldn't resist reviving the design, producing another 1,014 examples in its Moonshine gold alloy in 2019. But the originals have an appeal that can never quite be replicated, combining as they do mid-century space-age glamour with a whiff of Nixonian roguishness.

#6702. Men Are Outspending Women on Botox. Here’s What They’re Asking For

Guys are going to increasingly wild lengths to achieve their version of the idealized man. Looksmaxxing, as we’ve come to know it, has included surgical procedures to reshape their eyes, taking a hammer to their face to create a more chiseled jawline, and breaking their legs to gain a couple inches. But an OG in cosmetic procedures is seeing a resurgence in men, too.

Chances are you’re familiar with Botox: localized injections to smoothen wrinkles in common areas like the forehead, the eyes, or around the mouth. Long viewed as a treatment limited to the ladies, it’s increasingly becoming another form of male upkeep. Now we’re learning that men are outspending women on the popular procedure. According to an October 2026 report by Guidepoint Qsight, a medical aesthetics data and intelligence provider, men spent an average of $529 per neurotoxin visit in Q3 of 2026, compared with $455 for women. (The figures include not just Botox but also competitors like Daxxify and Xeomin.)

The potential explanation is simple: Botox is an easy way to look a little younger, more well-rested, and even less angry or severe. (As a built-in bonus, it’s virtually painless—especially compared to the barbaric looksmaxxing trends circulating as of late.) According to a 2025 survey of 2,900 aesthetic plastic surgeons, men accounted for over 15% of all Botox cases that year. It was also their top non-surgical treatment by a landslide.

The growing cohort of men opting into “Brotox” believe it’s money well spent. Nancy Samolitis, MD, a board-certified dermatologist in Los Angeles and co-founder of facile, has been treating men for years but has recently seen an uptick in male clientele, aged anywhere from 35 to 70. These days, “they’re more interested in skincare and more open to procedures,” she says. Many also book their first treatment nudged by their spouse or significant other who are existing patients. Others come in simply wanting to look better but not knowing what treatment to get. In many cases, Botox fits the bill.

While the uninitiated might think the injections make guys look surprised or less masculine, good work can be undetectable. Still you, just fresher.

Kyle Waldrop, a 37-year-old product designer in Los Angeles, first started getting Botox three years ago. “I’ve always been good about keeping up with a skincare routine and taking care of myself, so Botox felt like a natural extension of what I was already doing,” he says. His goal isn’t to change his face but to slow down wrinkles before they become obvious. He gets Botox for his forehead lines and crow’s feet, and recently started treating his neck lines, too.

But it’s not always about looking younger. Men are also using Botox in less obvious places. David Kim, MD, board-certified dermatologist and the founder of Soho Dermatology in New York City, treats the chin for more projection, as well as the underarms to combat excess sweating. Bell Yoo, NP, a board-certified nurse practitioner and the founder of Mimo Aesthetics in West Hollywood, adds that she injects the masseter (and sometimes temporalis) muscles for guys who struggle with jaw clenching or teeth grinding.

Aesthetically, men usually aren’t looking for the same thing from Botox as women. For starters, many want to soften their lines without erasing them. Preserving some natural movement is key. “My female patients often love that smooth, ‘glass skin’ appearance, whereas some of my male patients think it looks too waxy or oily,” Yoo says. Men also tend to opt out of a Botox brow lift, which many women request. “Men would much rather maintain a slightly heavier, lower-set brow than end up with an overly arched or ‘Spock’ brow,” Yoo says. Per Samolitis, this requires different injection points and doses tailored for guys. Kim, meanwhile, is careful not to overdo the crow’s feet. “It looks more natural when men have lines around the eyes, and it makes their face look better overall,” he says. These techniques quell the fears that guys have about looking less masculine or overdone. For most, the ultimate goal is to look relaxed and rejuvenated—not tight and taut. “When it’s done well and with the right amount, you look like yourself, just a bit more rested,” Waldrop says. “I still have all my natural movement and expressions, which is important to me.”

All that subtlety comes at a price. Men may end up paying more than women to treat the same areas, but the higher price tag is actually expected: Men often have larger, stronger facial muscles, which require more units to achieve their desired results, Yoo says.

While more men are embracing Botox, some remain tight-lipped about it. Yoo says that most of her male Botox patients are pretty discreet. “I think there’s still some stigma around men receiving cosmetic treatments, and many would rather keep it to themselves,” she says. Waldrop, however, is an open book. “I don’t really see it as something worth hiding or feeling that weird about,” he says. “Guys are already spending money on skincare, haircuts, hair transplants, working out, and all sorts of things to look and feel better, so I don’t really see why Botox should be treated any differently.”

For Waldrop, Botox is less a dramatic intervention than another item on his grooming checklist. He doesn’t see it as something that needs to be announced or apologized for. At the end of the day, “it’s really not that deep,” he says.

#6701. Tom Hardy’s Hair Through The Years—and How to Get Every Cut

Three decades, one head, a lot of styles. We’ll help you nail every look with advice from the pros.

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

You can track Tom Hardy’s life through his haircuts. The floppy, past-the-ears noughties look. The bald Bane era. His Peaky Blinders slick-back. Then there’s the fade he wore on The Revenant press tour and the short-back-and-sides he’s settled into today. He’s been on a proper hair journey over the past couple of decades and, somehow, he’s looked great on every stop along the way.

Really, he’s done exactly what every man should do when it comes to hair. You should never have the same haircut at 60 as when you’re 16, so changing it up is essential. Sure, some, for Hardy, were for his roles. He probably wouldn’t have gone for the full shaved look if he hadn’t been cast as a masked supervillain who breaks Batman’s back. But, even so, his go-to now is a long way from the boyband look he wore when he was younger.

So, we asked some of London’s best barbers how to get each cut, as well as who each one suits. If you’re anything like Hardy, you’ll suit all six.

The haircut: Think of this as the slick-back’s more laid-back cousin. “It's a classic textured scissor cut with a low, natural finish,” says Robyn Mulgrew, a senior barber at Ruffians. “It’s kept soft around the ears and nape, with the sides relatively full and enough length to sweep it back.” No harsh fades or sharp lines, just a cut that looks like it grew naturally—and it’s a versatile shape that works for everything from oval to square and round faces.

What to ask for: Mulgrew’s advice? Keep it simple: “Don’t overcomplicate it, or you’ll confuse each other,” she says. “Use these key words: textured scissor cut with a low natural taper, swept-back top, styled to give a soft, lived-in finish.” All you need to do now is read it out with the help of a Hardy reference photo—job done.

How to style it: The idea is for your hair to look naturally lifted rather than quiffed. “I'd prep with Ruffians Beach Salt Spray, then blow-dry it in, sweeping the hair back and up,” says Mulgrew. “Keep the sides flatter so it still looks smart, then finish with out Matt Clay or Beach Fix for some texture.” Think swept-back beach hair, just the commuter’s version.

The haircut: Short, sharp, and no messing about, the soft buzz is for the guys who are confident with clippers. “This is pretty close to a buzz cut, approximately a grade three on top and a grade two on the sides, with a very subtle taper around the edges,” says Maxwell Oakley, co-founder of luxury London barbershop Supply 91. Fair warning, though: there’s nowhere to hide. “It’s quite an exposing haircut, so it works particularly well on someone with a strong hairline,” he says. Square and oval faces with a strong jawline suit it best—but Oakley’s not one for hard rules: “If you wear something with enough confidence, you can pull anything off.”

What to ask for: “Ask your barber to use clippers rather than scissors,” says Oakley. “A grade three on top and a grade two on the sides, with a subtle taper through the edges; you could also mention to lightly break into the fringe with the clippers to keep the front looking natural and textured.” Though it might seem simple, it’s so precise that you need someone who knows exactly what they’re doing—so always stick to a good barbershop.

How to style it: Less is more. “Avoid anything wet or greasy, as that can make the hair look sparse,” says Oakley. “Keep things matte to bring out the natural texture, with just a little bit of hold; work a small amount of product through for some definition without weighing it down.” Basically, a tiny bit of paste rubbed between your palms and through your hair, and you’re out the door before you can say “buzz cut.”

The haircut: Brent Pankhurst has been cutting hair for more than 30 years, from training under Vidal Sassoon to his shop in the Thom Sweeney townhouse in Mayfair, so he knows a good crop when he sees one. "It’s disheveled elegance,” he says. “It has a relaxed, slightly rugged feel, but the foundation is a strong, carefully considered shape.” It suits most faces, too, as long as the proportions are tailored to you. “For gentlemen approaching their forties and beyond, I often prefer a little more length through the sides for a softer, less harsh finish,” Pankhurst adds.

What to ask for: It all starts with the temples: “The most important thing with this haircut—and, for me, every man’s haircut—is to cut a square shape through the temple area,” says Pankhurst. Ask for scissors throughout, with scissor-over-comb on the back and sides, which “gives the haircut better structure and helps it hold its shape as it grows.” You don’t want to be going to the barbers every couple of weeks, after all. The top should be cut with texture for movement and separation; if your hair is thick, ask for more texturing to take out some bulk.

How to style it: Work some styling conditioner through damp hair, then give it a quick blow-dry using your fingers. Finish with a nickel-sized amount of molding cream. “Spread it evenly between your palms, then work it vigorously through the hair for that rugged, disheveled finish,” says Pankhurst.

The haircut: The slick-back, minus the Wall Street energy. “This is a textured slick-back or flow cut,” says Chris Merrick, head barber at Murdock Soho. “Keeping it relaxed rather than tightly slicked allows the natural movement and texture to come through.” Basically, it should look like you’ve just run your hands through it rather than set it hours earlier. It suits most faces, too. “It works particularly well on oval, square and rounder face shapes; styling the hair back opens up the face and highlights the cheekbones, while some height on top can help elongate a rounder face.”

What to ask for: “Ask your barber for a textured slick-back or flow cut, with the sides slightly shorter than the top but enough length throughout to push the hair back,” says Merrick. “You want to retain movement and avoid a traditional short back and sides.”

How to style it: Towel dry your hair after showering, leaving it slightly damp, then work in a sea salt spray. “Blow-dry while using your fingers to guide the hair backwards and encourage natural movement,” says Merrick. Finish with a small amount of Murdock Matt Mud, worked between your hands and through the hair for a strong hold with a matte finish.

The haircut: Think of this as the haircut equivalent of rolling out of bed and looking like you meant to. “This is a classic textured cut with a messy, tousled finish,” says Guray Kesman, founder of luxury barbershop Kesman Grooming, “and it’s got that effortlessly undone look that Tom Hardy wears so well.” It’s also one of the most versatile cuts going: the fringe softens the forehead, so it suits oval, square, and longer faces, and it works best on straight to slightly wavy hair with some thickness.

What to ask for: “Ask for a scissor-cut textured crop, keeping the sides and back shorter but avoiding a harsh skin fade,” says Kesman. “You’ll want a good amount of length and movement through the top, with the fringe left slightly longer. The key is asking your barber to add texture rather than cutting everything too uniformly, so the hair has that relaxed, lived-in finish.”

How to style it: Work a small amount of lightweight styling cream or matte paste through slightly damp hair, then rough-dry it, directing the fringe forwards and slightly to one side. “Use your fingers to separate a few pieces,” says Kesman. “You don’t want it looking too polished or overly styled; a little movement is what makes this cut work.” When it looks like you’ve spent as little time as possible trying to perfect it, you’ve nailed it.

The haircut: If you were around in the ’90s, chances are you’re familiar with curtains—and you probably have the school photos to prove it. “To me, Tom Hardy’s curtains were something of an homage to Jonny Depp,” says Jay Hecker, master barber at Truefitt & Hill, the world’s oldest barbershop based in St. James’s, London. “We've seen this look before—it was everywhere when Brad Pitt broke though with Thelma & Louise and when David Beckham wore it in the ’90s—and you can trace it right back to old Hollywood’s Robert Mitchum.” Now, it’s making a comeback—and it suits most face shapes. “Because the hair is tucked behind the ears, face shape isn’t too much of a factor,” continues Hecker. “And if you want to accentuate your cheekbones, it’s definitely one to consider.”

What to ask for: “Ask for layers at the back to take out any weight, and keep most of the length on top so you can tuck it behind your ears,” says Hecker. A center part takes care of the rest. If you’re growing it out from a shorter cut, tell your barber so they can shape it in stages—just make sure you take a visual reference so you’re both on the same page.

How to style it: “Apply a cream like our C.A.R. cream or Circassian Cream to towel-dried hair,” says Hecker. “Then use a diffuser on your hairdryer on medium to low speed and heat for that textured finish.” The C.A.R. Cream has some history, too: Truefitt & Hill made it in the early 1900s for members of the Royal Automobile Club to keep their hair in check while driving open-top cars. Your day at the office, then, will probably be safe.
