#6698. With the ‘New’ Yeezy, Ye Says He Wants to ‘Let the Clothes Do the Talking.’ But Who’s Listening?

“I don’t have a lot of words tonight,” Ye told me, not long after concluding his Yeezy Season 01 runway show in Los Angeles on Wednesday evening. “I’ll let the clothes do the talking.”

You might read that quote and be surprised to hear that Ye said it warmly, with an all-32-bared smile. But for those still paying attention, his relatively upbeat demeanor tracks with the tame-not-turnt persona that Ye has performed through most of 2026; the one that’s been slowly-but-surely trying to reamass the cultural capital he’s spent most of the last decade either annihilating or reclaiming. Legions of fans have proven they’re still all in; the culture at large remains justifiably dubious. The question this week is, how will Ye’s return to fashion affect the calculus?

Upon arrival, a typical Hollywood warehouse exterior turned into a Frank Herbert-esque interior as soon as you crossed the threshold. Inside, a hundred or so guests sat on two rows of unbroken benches that encircled a large white mound in the center, lit by a heavenly white glow from above. Inside was a mix of the usual cool kids: models (including the runway-favorite teen rapper Nettspend) and recurring characters in the overarching, enduring Kanye Universe. There was Fear of God founder Jerry Lorenzo, who made conversation with Don C, while 2 Chainz was patiently posted up in a hooded black leather coat like ’98 Wesley Snipes. Big Sean, rebuffing the sweltering indoor temperatures in a shearling jacket over a crewneck sweater, caught up with Hit-Boy.

Models on the Yeezy Season 01 runway, which featured a characteristically minimalist set piece.

The 19-year-old rapper Nettspend walked in the show.

Once everyone was seated, the lights dimmed as the unmistakable opening piano note of “Runaway” set the show into motion, with one lone model striding out in a killer black trench. From there, two more models, men this time, walked out in the opposite direction; what followed was a bit of organized chaos with the number of models and paths alternating at random intervals to the tune of a playlist that zipped between new Ye music (UK garage vibes, it would seem) and a gothic orchestra not unlike the scores Danny Elfman used to chef up for Tim Burton.

The fits were crisp, practical and tactical. Most of the menswear was ready-made for basic maneuvers, while the women’s looks were sleek and casual, with a few Milla Jovovich-in-Fifth-Element skimpy ensembles thrown in because Ye can only restrain himself but so much. The most notable thing, about the guys especially, were the relatively streamlined silhouettes: close-cropped coats, bombers and vests, fitted pants and sweats. Where past Ye lines championed and emblazoned the path for the baggy wave, with oversized boots and pants you could swim in, Ye seemed to be brazenly planting a flag back on the opposite end of the spectrum.

Nearly 12 years, and a dozen lifetimes, since his debut Yeezy Season 1 runway show in 2015, the notion of a “Season 01”—his first formal showing since his damning 2022 show in Paris, the one with the “White Lives Matter” shirts—is a conspicuous attempt to clear the slate. All of his previous shows had been produced in collaboration with Adidas, who severed ties with Ye four years ago, after he tweeted a tirade of antisemetic remarks. This time around, to let the clothes tell it, Ye is locked in. I don’t have an image of every single Yeezy Season line seared into my brain, but I feel comfortable saying Ye’s latest was the first time I looked at one of his collections and objectively thought, I could wear that. Most of the gear is quite solid—and familiar Ye's running the same playbook with Season 01 that he’s run with his music, and to some degree, his persona. It’s the Yeezy gear you know and love(d), refined to make it that much more palatable, similar to the way a song like “Whatever Works” puts him back over chipmunk’d soul samples. And true to the title, it does indeed; the fact that it does is maybe a testament to the brilliance that got lost in the insanity. But is playing the hits (quite literally, with “Runaway” opening the show) what we want from one of the most paradigm-shifting—and absolutely chaotic—creative minds of our time?

Historically, Ye’s designs are so focused on an idea of utilitarianism that they ultimately come off as a bit drab, if not downright dystopian. Essentials like hoodies and beefy leather jackets are still central to his vision, but as the models strode around in his usual earth-toned palette—save for a handful of black looks to start, and a bright pop of reds at the end—it became clear that Ye was putting on the same show in a different kind of stadium. The same sci-fi silhouettes, tweaked to their cleanest shape and most pleasing hue, sans screenprinted hate speech. Ideologically, the runway show wasn’t far off from the experience of hearing his 2007 hit “Good Life” all these years later, ringing off stadium speakers, carrying 80,000 people along on a wave of wistfulness— even if Ye himself only performed approximately 75% of the lyrics against a backing track.

In keeping with all of his other moves this year, Yeezy Season 01 is Ye presenting a safer, rule-abiding version of himself: Ye with just art, no antics. Much in the way that his latest album, Bully, eschewed any lyrics that say, shouted out R. Kelly or Bill Cosby (which is more than can be said about 2024’s Vultures 1), it felt like a deep sigh of relief to see him present clothes that don’t feature appalling slogans, at a fashion show with Candace Owens nowhere in sight. But the compromise for a sanitized Kanye is also one that can’t help but feel anodyne as well. The New Ye doesn’t needle, but he’s lost Old Ye’s knack for trying something new. Of course, we’re also well over a decade into the Yeezy experiment—at this point, the present has caught up to the futuristic vision Ye first dreamt up back in 2015. There’s a whole new generation of aspiring designers creating in his image, kids who fancy themselves trailblazers whose paths just loop back to Ye: streamer Kai Cenat just launched his own clothing brand, Vivet, while N3on attended Wednesday night’s show, skipping the Yeezy space gleefully documenting every interaction. It feels as though all of this has happened before, and all of it will happen again.

Mystery. Intrigue. Chaos. This is the typical life cycle for a Ye project, be it music, fashion, or otherwise. An announcement materializes, hype ensues. Then, inevitably, the seams start to show and it becomes apparent that he isn’t inviting you on a pre-planned journey so much as building the plane in real time, seconds before takeoff. And then, through sheer force of will, or maybe even genius, the result ends up so captivating that the speedbumps hardly matter. He’s still a master showman, and all it takes is a strong yearning for the Old Ye to find yourself surrendering to it. The efficacy of that last factor, though, has undeniably waned over the years. For much of the 2020s, in the midst of many a manic outburst or worse, Ye’s messes have hardly cohered into something objectively beautiful. The intrigue curdled into perverse bemusement; for every unwavering diehard fan, there was a rubbernecker tapped in to watch the circus.

The 2026 version of Ye has been an exercise in proving how many people will show up when he shuts up, and whether he can make it back from the brink if he delivers the right spectacle.

The result is the most well-oiled the Yeezy Machine has ever been. An album that more or less dropped when he said it would, for once. A smattering of live shows across the world, each in a sold-out stadium boasting record-breaking rap concert attendance where he leans on his most enduring strengths: visual spectacle and a bulletproof catalog. The only area left to renegotiate is fashion…which brings us back to Wednesday night in LA.

These are the scenes you’d expect at a Ye event, but the first sign that the overall vibe was different and more relaxed was that the man himself was at the door, tucked off into a hallway, immaculately dressed in a fitted all-black leather fit offset by a ridiculously large and ornate gold waist fixture—every bit the Dune-caliber fit as the space we were standing in required. Ahead of the show, he posed backstage flanked by his wife, Bianca Censori, and the four kids he shares with his ex, Kim Kardashian (including 13-year-old North, who was sporting her unmistakable blue-raspberry locks). At Yeezy events past, you’d expect to see harried staff scrambling, while Ye was somewhere out of sight but exuding stress and anxiety out into the atmosphere. But on Wednesday, the 49-year-old beamed as friends made their way past him to their seats. Even a three-piece-suited Kid Cudi came over to say hello.

Ye backstage with his children, from left: North, Psalm, Chicago, and Saint West.

Fifteen years ago, Yeezy Season 01 would’ve caused a massive sea change in the menswear world almost instantaneously, with the wave starting in SoHo then slowly emanating out into the beyond. In 2026, does Kanye still move that needle? The Year in Ye has been one big display of his ability to Don Draper the culture at large, and shock us all by “how much it never happened.” There’s no controversy big enough to keep him away for too long and very few fences he can’t mend.

For example, Sean and Cudi ceased their umpteenth falling out with their old label boss two months ago at a guest-packed, blockbuster homecoming show in Chicago on Ye’s tour stop; their show of support tonight only underlined how deeply the hatchet has been buried yet again. When I spoke to Big Sean after the show, he could only marvel at how much Ye has “given” to the culture across all different categories. One-time GOOD Music COO Steven Victor said the show made him feel like Ye “has his shit together,” noting that Ye admitted to him that he was still working out the model patterns hours before showtime. In past Yeezy Seasons, the cram-effect would’ve been apparent, but here, Victor said he wouldn’t have noticed because it all seemed to go off so effortlessly.

As for the man himself? I spoke to him, but again, he didn’t have much to say, at least on the record. He seemed excited that I picked up on the intentional disharmony of the model routes, and explained that the space we were standing in was a “language I’ve been working on for 10 years” called “DROAM,” which refers to the minimalist design he hopes to apply to stores, urban planning, and a home for himself.

“I got a quote for you,” Victor interjected. “The king returns!” Ye laughed. The question remains, though: Is he welcome?

#6695. John Apparently Brought an Escort Home During ‘Love Is Blind’ Cheating Scandal

The only marriage from season 11 has already come crashing down.

John Shannon and Sarah Williams were the only Bostonians to walk down the aisle on Love Is Blind season 11, but their marriage has unfortunately already come to an end. During the reality show’s reunion special, John revealed he “made a mistake” and committed a “major breach of trust” after a “wonderful year of marriage.”

According to John’s own admission, he had “crossed a line” when he secretly exchanged graphic texts with a stripper and escort while he was still married to Sarah.

“I don’t blame her at all for leaving,” John said during the reunion. “It’s been really hard.”

Now, the account executive is facing new allegations, making his cheating scandal even messier.

According to Rikki Wise and Ashley Thomson’s Reality Receipts podcast, John apparently did more than just text the escort. In a separate video Rikki shared, one of her sources alleged that John brought the other woman home.

“I am told that John did invite this stripper slash escort back to his Airbnb, and the only reason nothing physical happened is because he did not want to pay her,” Rikki reported, adding, “I’m not sure if she actually went back and then payment was discussed or if the discussion happened in the text messages.”

She continued, “What I do know is that John apparently thought that him and the stripper had a connection. So he felt like he didn’t need to pay her because he thought it was an organic connection and not a paid transaction.”

Rikki clarified that further details about the alleged text messages between John and the other woman haven’t been verified, so take it all with a grain of salt.

Morrey Elgin, who butted heads with John since he also connected with Sarah in the Love Is Blind pods, also weighed in on the scandal. He made a dig at John’s infidelity with a playful Instagram video where he skims through pages of the Bible.

“I just can’t seem to find the chapter where it says you can cheat on your wife,” he said in the video as he grabbed the book. Ouch.

Sarah and John have yet to comment on Rikki’s reported intel, so stay tuned for more.

#6694. 21 Coats and Jackets I Think Are Cool Just Because

No contest, fall is the best season. For a few glorious months, it’s an open bar for dressing and getting your biggest fits off. Tell me what other season lets you go from flannel with cords to a hoodie with shorts to a puffer and jeans all in the same week. You can mix a wild cocktail of textures and colors, play recklessly with silhouettes and experiment to your heart’s content—and your wallet’s demise. But no doubt the best part of dressing for the best season is the outerwear.

And right now, the shelves are stocked with sick pieces, which means my tabs are so more packed than a Tokyo train during rush hour. From dramatic overcoats to slick truckers, artisanal macs and swishy windbreakers, the menswear gods are really smiling upon us right now. But why gab about how great this season’s roster of jackets and coats are when I can just show you? Here are the 21 coolest pieces of outerwear out right now.

Little Red Riding Hood was a menswear icon.

I didn't know you could wax corduroy. Now I do. And now I want it.

After decades of wearing clothes, I'm pretty convinced that reversible jackets are bad. Leave it to Niceness to be the very sick outlier.

This isn't totally my style but I'll figure out a way to make it work because it's just too cool to pass up.

Our Legacy just seems to be getting better and better.

I'm obsessed with Nosapluna, the mysterious and methodical design collective producing antique-inspired designs using artisanal and age-old techniques. Like, who needs Gore-tex when you have this?

Imagine this with a hoodie underneath and crazy faded baggy jeans. Pretty good.

Hoodies aren't normally categorized as outerwear. But then again, hoodies aren't normally as heavy as this, nor are they hand-knit by indigenous craftspeople. So, yeah.

Experiences are great. But if I have to choose between paying to fly to Montana or paying to LARP Montana, I'm going with this RRL jacket every time.

I did a review of Gap's heavy denim Type II jacket a while back and got accused of sponsored content. I meant that shit. And I mean this too: this is a pretty decent jacket that looks good and is reasonably priced.

It appears that the Dries team is doing just fine without Dries.

Boston-based indie label Mark the Tailor has the streets talking thanks to its balance of craftsmanship and a modern lens on tailoring. This playful flip on the humble quarter zip is just one of the things that has menswear fans buzzing.

Enough pockets for a 6-pack of Labatt Blue.

I'll be the first to say the pullover jackets are, on the whole, stupid. But there are always notable exceptions, like this sweet one from A Kind of Guise.

You could pay for top dollar for an authentic, vintage thrashed French chore coat. Or you could pay top dollar for a new, equally nice, hand-stitched French chore coat made specifically for the Self Edge 20th anniversary. The choice is yours.

Vintage heads will recognize this Buck piece as a retro revival of '30s jackets with its cropped silhouette, chain-zip chest pocket, and waist adjusters. The only thing it's missing is that vintage '30s aroma.

If the Chunnel were a garment, it would be this collaborative coat between A.P.C. and Barbour.

#6690. Alix Earle Quietly Supports Sofia Franklyn’s ‘Call Her Daddy’ Tell-All Book Amid Rumored Rift With Alex Cooper

Sofia Franklyn and Alex Cooper’s public falling out over Call Her Daddy may have gone down six years ago, but the drama seems to be alive and well, since the Sofia With an F host dropped a LOT more details in her new tell-all memoir, Daddy Issues.

She first announced Daddy Issues back in February, exclusively telling Rolling Stone, “The decision [to write the book] was one I came to on my own. Six years ago, I was reeling from an experience I didn’t understand. I needed to grow up and be free of ego as much as humanly possible.”

Alix Earle, who’s had her own beef with Alex ever since the Unwell Network dropped her Hot Mess podcast in February 2025, made sure to not-so-subtly support Sofia’s book on social media, posting a TikTok of herself reading Daddy Issues. Oh, and she also poked fun at that Interview magazine Q&A where she claimed she’s “never read a book,” adding text over her video that reads, “There’s a first time for everything!!!”

In case you aren’t aware of the layers this simple move has—and are still asking yourself, Wait, Alex Cooper and Alix Earle aren’t the same person?—allow us to catch you up to speed.

Sofia—who hosted Call Her Daddy with her bestie and college roommate Alex Cooper under Dave Portnoy’s company, Barstool—had a public falling-out in 2020 after they disagreed on negotiations with Barstool over the future of the show. While Sofia’s boyfriend at the time, Peter Nelson (aka “Suitman”), advised the girls not to renew their Barstool contract and take the show elsewhere, Alex wanted to sign on for one more year under the condition that she and Sofia owned the intellectual property of the pod.

In 2021, Alex continued Call Her Daddy without Sofia, eventually signing a $60 million deal with Spotify and another $125 million deal with SiriusXM in 2024. The latter includes exclusive advertising and distribution rights for Call Her Daddy, as well as the Unwell Network, her company.

Which brings us to Alex’s rumored rift with Alix Earle. When developing the Unwell Network, Alex signed the TikTok creator as one of the first podcasters under the company with Alix’s show, Hot Mess, in August 2023.

Things seemed to be going well until this time last year, when Variety reported that the Hot Mess podcast was dropped by the Unwell Network amid feud rumors between the social media personalities.

Sources gave a bit more context to the Alix vs. Alex situation with Us Weekly, saying, “They’ve had a lot of drama between them [since] they decided to collaborate on Unwell. They clashed over business, and their friendship is over because of it.”

While Alix has seemingly taken a hiatus from podcasting, Alex specified that “Alix not being able to podcast has nothing to do with Unwell” in a March 2025 TikTok post. She added that “Unwell gave her everything back” and that Alix owns her intellectual property.

As for Daddy Issues, Sofia also told Rolling Stone, “A book is reflective, vulnerable; it can’t be performative. The writing practice has been intense—sitting down, laying everything bare—but probably the most enriching thing I’ve ever done.”

#6688. Our 11 Favorite Watches Coming to the Biggest Auctions of the Year

Want more insider watch coverage? Get Box + Papers, GQ's newsletter devoted to the watch world, sent to your inbox every Friday. Sign up here to get it free.

This is the biggest weekend of the year for vintage watches. It’s the year-end watch auctions in Geneva, where many prestigious houses bring their very best pieces to market. And it’s never been a more enthralling time to keep a close eye on what happens in Geneva over the next few days. In the first half of the year, the number of million-dollar watches nearly eclipsed the total for the entirety of 2025. A couple of the pieces listed below, including a unique Rolex and a rare Patek Philippe in stainless steel, have estimates above a million bucks.

Writer Oren Hartov and I pored over hundreds of lots to bring you our 11 favorite watches plus one bonus selection. See them all below.

You know a banger is coming up for sale when the house behind it doesn’t even bother capping its price range. Auction estimates are routinely and famously low to encourage bidders, and this watch’s starting point is still $1.2 million. Why? Well, have you ever seen a Rolex like this before? Before Rolex defined the look of modern watches with pieces like the Submariner, it operated similarly to many other manufacturers in Switzerland, willing to make one-off pieces and experiment frequently with its design.

This square beauty from the early 1940s is “most probably unique,” as Phillips auction house writes in its essay on the piece. This exact piece last hit the auction block in 2004, and no other example has come up for public sale since. It’s crazy to see how much this watch’s case shape resembles Heuer’s similarly blocky Monaco, which wouldn’t be released for another 25-plus years. Unfortunately, as cool as this watch is, it does not come with onyx prayer beads. —Cam Wolf

There’s Patek, then there’s complicated Patek, then there’s stainless steel complicated Patek, then there’s stainless steel complicated Patek made in just a handful of examples, then there’s stainless steel complicated Patek made in just a handful of examples in insane condition. This watch checks all those boxes. A reference 1436, it’s a split-seconds (rattrapante) chronograph, meaning it can time successive intervals of a race or event. One of just two stainless steel examples known to the market, it has an estimate of $2.4-$4.8M, a testament to its rarity and importance. —Oren Hartov

Yet another rare watch executed in stainless steel, this fabulous Lange 1 boasts a gorgeous two-tone dial in blue, with darker subdials contrasting against a cobalt-colored main dial. Dating to 1999 and first sold by the German retailer Oeding-Erdel, it’s one of approximately 30 pieces made in the most utilitarian of metals. Originally fitted with a more standard silvered dial, it received its current navy-blue dial as a replacement and is one of just two stainless steel Lange 1s known in this particular configuration. —OH

Renewed interest in Urban Jürgensen stemming from its recent relaunch under the directorship of Kari Voutilainen, who has since left the CEO position but remains on the company’s board, has seen watches such as this early-’90s ref. 2 garner more attention. A lovely perpetual calendar-moonphase, it’s housed in a classically inspired 37.7mm yellow gold case with cornes-de-vache-style lugs, a stepped bezel, and an incredible dial with the brand’s signature observatory-style hands. Someone tell Timothée Chalamet. —OH

Chopard has a hard-earned reputation for putting out some of the most finely made watches in Switzerland. It’s not just the nerds who are familiar with its work anymore, either. If we (the nerds, obviously) were sending a watch in a bottle to help share that message far and wide, this Lunar One, which looks like something out of Superman’s icy Fortress of Solitude, wouldn’t be a bad choice. This is Chopard’s first watch to combine a perpetual calendar and a moonphase, which is like shaving truffles on caviar. I love that the Roman numerals at 4 and 8 o’clock look like columns nearly buckling under the weight of the center of the dial. This watch also comes with several fun add-ons, like onyx prayer beads plus a ring and a pen set with diamonds. —Cam Wolf

While many younger collectors are obsessed with the Cosmograph Daytona, true “Crown” heads know that other pre-Daytona chronographs sometimes present an even more compelling proposition. This ref. 3525 ‘’Bariletto,’ dating to the 1940s, is not only the first Oyster-cased Rolex chronograph, but it’s also the only known stainless-steel example carrying the signature on the dial of Proud’s, a Rolex retailer based in Sydney. Manually wound, paired to a handsome beads-of-rice bracelet, and measuring a compact 36mm, it comes from the collection of important Italian collector Pucci Papaleo. —OH

While this stainless steel Patek may look like a normal ref. 2509—a beautiful hand-wound dress watch from the midcentury—it’s actually a “school watch” modified by a watchmaking student at the Geneva Watchmaking School. Dating to the 1960s, it’s housed in a 35mm stainless steel case and features a lovely dial with the ‘Ecole d’Horlogerie Geneve’ signature instead of Patek Philippe’s. Open it up, and the hand-wound movement is signed by Norbert Henry, the watchmaking student who assembled and finished it. While it may not attract the attention of more typical Patek collectors, its under-the-radar qualities make it a potential sleeper hit. —OH

It’s tough to argue with the sheer beauty of Vacheron’s mid-20th-century output, much of which is vastly underappreciated compared to comparable pieces from the likes of Patek Philippe. This splendid yellow gold ref. 4072 with a two-tone multi-scale dial dates to the late 1940s—one of my favorite eras of watch design. Powered by the hand-wound cal. V 492 movement and featuring a lovely set of long, contoured lugs, its dial is in fantastic condition, with applied dart and Roman indices, sharp printing, a crisp signature, and legible tachymeter and telemeter scales. It will surely find its way onto the wrist of a collector with excellent taste. —OH

This watch’s dial depicts Saint George, who, according to legend, slew a dragon while on horseback. This is a perfect use for the rich, deep colors you get from enamel. The flames licking at Saint George are beautiful shades of orange and red. He triumphantly lifts his spear as his steed rears its front legs. It’s very cool how much story you can get from this little 35mm timepiece. —CW

Watch collectors might pony up a few thousand for one of independent watchmaker F.P. Journe’s used tissues at this point. However, they’ll have to settle for a host of accessories coming to auction instead this weekend. The maker is so in-demand that, in addition to his timepieces, houses are selling his empty watch boxes and a set of toys that resemble him. This lot is in the middle of those two extremes: a wall clock made to resemble Journe’s Octa Réserve de Marche. Although I’m not gonna lie, flipping this over and seeing the compartment where you stuff in the batteries is a bit of a buzzkill. —CW

They used to make everything look more elegant. This original Longines design was developed in 1931 with famous aviator Charles Lindbergh. It is technically a pilot’s watch, but its elegant, icy-blue dial and Roman numerals would make it the fanciest model on many red carpets in 2026. This is a model from the ‘80s inspired by that original design, and it comes on an absolutely righteous beads-of-rice bracelet. Someone is going to get this guy for around $2,500 if the estimate is correct! —CW

In Rolex world, even the advertisements sell for thousands of dollars. It’s hard to blame people when we’re talking about a rare poster of a GMT-Master wearing a snow-dusted Rolex beanie. Awwww. (Relatedly, imagine if Rolex sold that beanie!? Oh, boy.) —CW

#6687. Mocha Brown Hair-Color Season Is Here—and It’s Universally Flattering

There’s something about this time of year that makes everybody want to go darker. From their nail polish to their wardrobe, it’s a turn toward rich, darker shades. So it’s only natural that hair color would follow suit, going deeper and darker too. This winter in particular, it’s all about mocha brown.

Also called mocha chocolate, the rich, glossy shade was first popularized by celebrity colorist Jenna Perry, whose multidimensional brunette wizardry has fans like Bella Hadid and Kaia Gerber. Since then, the smooth and chocolaty hue—which combines darker espresso tones with lighter, milkier brunette shades—has become popular with a much wider section of the A-list, with Hailey Bieber and Daisy Edgar-Jones among the latest devotees.

“It reminds me of a chocolate mousse or a rich chocolate cake,” Perry tells Vogue. “That’s where the name came from. I thought mocha chocolate resembled the decadence of this shade of brown.” In line with sweater weather, Perry has churned out individualized takes on what she calls the “universally flattering brunette hair color” for a slew of stars, including longtime client Emily Ratajkowski and Amelia Gray. “A deep, rich brunette is chic,” Perry says. “The color reflects warmly in the sun, so it doesn’t ever look drab.”

According to Stephen Buller and Anita Rice, founders of Buller and Rice salon, these cacao tones feel luxurious for winter, particularly when the color isn’t completely flat. The key? Having a varied tone. “This is important throughout the lengths and ends so it reflects light and feels expensive rather than looking like one solid block of dark color,” they say. “This also gives clients who have had years of balayage an opportunity to go darker without feeling as though they’re simply covering everything up.”

Celebrity hairstylist Lorraine Dublin says that these indulgent, chocolaty shades look especially beautiful on textured and curly hair: “Think deep espresso brown with subtle ribbons of mocha, creating a rich, multidimensional finish,” she previously told Vogue. “It works beautifully on textured hair because the tonal variations catch the light, highlighting the shape and movement of curls, coils, and kinks.” She recommends asking your colorist for a “rich espresso base” with “delicate mocha or cinnamon brown accents,” which will impart a soft, seamless color melt.

Another important part of mastering the mocha chocolate look is keeping hair strong and shiny. “What makes this hair color so incredible from the start is having healthy hair,” explains Perry, who adds that preserving pigments with a color-safe shampoo and conditioner is key. Try Matrix Total Results Brass Off Shampoo to nix brassy or red tones. You can also use an at-home gloss or color conditioner, like Glaze Supergloss Color Conditioning Hair Gloss Chocolate Gleam or John Frieda Brilliant Brunette Color Vibrancy Conditioner, to prolong your hair’s vibrancy.

If a rich shade of mocha chocolate sounds like a delectable prospect, discover even more mocha brown hair-color inspiration to take to your salon below.

#6685. From UGGs to Cowboy Boots, These Are the Boots Worth Building Outfits Around

When temperatures drop, boots become the foundation of every great outfit. And the secret to great fall and winter dressing? Let your boots set the tone, then build the outfit around them. Whether you're going Western, rain, knee-high, or platform, the right pair will make every cold-weather look feel effortlessly stylish.

If you need some inspiration to start building those looks, I’ve got you covered. Here are a few of my favorite boots to lean into during the fall and winter months.

UGG boots are the ultimate cozy-girl essential. Style mini UGGs with leggings, slouchy socks, an oversized sweatshirt, and a puffer jacket for an off-duty model look à la Bella Hadid. You can also pair platform UGGs with relaxed straight-leg jeans, a fitted knit, and a longline wool coat.

Moto boots are a very popular style when you don’t want something as sleek as a pointed-toe knee-high boot, but still want to feel a little feminine. They pair well with mini skirts and are the perfect time to get adventurous with color blocking and prints.

A good bootie will have you feeling like you can take on the world. They’re the ideal boot for the in-between fall and winter outfit moments, especially if you’re not feeling like having your legs completely covered. They pair perfectly with a good pair of jorts like the one above, creating perfect proportions.

Western and Cowboy boots are the ultimate transitional staple. I personally love to wear them with a good pair of denim, like the image above. You can also style a classic brown or black pair with a mini skirt, oversized knit sweater, and sheer tights for an effortlessly cool fall look.

There’s something so chic yet everyday about Hunter boots. Whether you actually need them for practical rain and snow use, or if just for the fashion moment, they go great with all your fall looks.

A slouchy boot adds drama to any look you are wearing. Whether ankle-length or more knee-high, they go great with skirts, tucked-in jeans, and even jorts depending on the moment. They’re a fun addition to your boot collection and I suggest everyone get a pair.

Aiyana Ishmael is the style editor at Cosmopolitan magazine. In her work, Aiyana focuses on the culture of fashion and how it intertwines and shapes the zeitgeist. She is an award-winning journalist from Miami, Florida, and a graduate of the historically Black university, Florida A&M. She is a 2024 Forbes 30 Under 30: Media honoree, and her debut romance novella PASSING GAME is set to release March of 2027 (831 Stories/Simon & Schuster).

#6682. GQ’s 13 Breakout Stars of 2026

Sometimes it’s fun to be early to the party. GQ launched the Breakouts portfolio in 2014 to celebrate the Hollywood upstarts who were poised to do big things. And not to brag, but we nailed it; that first group included Miles Teller, Shailene Woodley, Eddie Redmayne, and the late, great Chadwick Boseman. Over the ensuing years, we featured plenty more soon-to-be-superstars: Rami Malek. Brie Larson. Daniel Kaluuya. Riz Ahmed. Barry Keoghan. The kids from Stranger Things, when they were still kids. The cast of Succession before Succession became everyone’s favorite show.

In 2017, we put a baby-faced Timothée Chalamet on the list for his turn in Call Me by Your Name. (Our global entertainment director, Dana Mathews, fondly recalls him turning to her during the Man of the Year Party and saying, “This is the best party I’ve ever been to in my life.”) Since then, Chalamet has appeared on three GQ covers, earned four Oscar nominations, and become a household name (and maybe been to a few more great parties).

We took a few years off (okay, fine, seven), but 2026 feels like exactly the right time to revive GQ’s Breakouts portfolio—now through a wider lens. Yes, though we’ve got eyes on Hollywood’s next big things—like, say, Sly Stallone doppelgänger Anthony Ippolito; Gen Z class clown Owen Thiele, of Adults; Misty Green’s astounding leading woman, Rosalind Eleazar—not everyone is working the camera. We’re excited to introduce you to the West Coast’s hottest up-and-coming rapper in AZ Chike and put you on to Fcukers, whose debut album, packed with rowdy electro-pop bangers, made them first in our hearts and on our pregaming playlists. We’ve also got podcaster and journalist Pablo Torre, whose relentless reporting upended a $7.8 billion NBA franchise, and Jordan Firstman, an internet-born comedian who wrote and directed a film that commanded a lengthy standing ovation from Cannes audiences (right after they finished crying).

Point is, our Breakouts class of 2026 brings together 13 fully minted stars who did something that feels increasingly impossible: They captured our attention in a time when it feels like attention is more fleeting than ever. Welcome to their party.

Dress by Dior. Shoes by Jimmy Choo. Mask from Palace Costume Co.

Age: 25Breakout moment: Playing the queen bee on Ryan Murphy’s ’80s glam fever dream The Shards.—————

It’s a different kind of attention that I actually prefer. Because for so long I was getting attention, but not for a specific thing. And that was confusing and kind of messed with my head a bit. I was really young and people knew who I was, but I couldn’t really point to the work that they knew. And so it’s really fun for me to have people [now say], “I watched The Shards,” or “I watched Bottoms,” and then we have something to talk about.

Infinite Jest—no, I’m kidding, they’ve already read it, all of them. A Man’s Place by Annie Ernaux is very good and easily digestible. I think Just Kids is wonderful. What We Talk About When We Talk About Love. That one’s really great. Oh, and this is so classic, but Nine Stories by J.D. Salinger is really good. I know guys—male actors especially—love The Catcher in the Rye.

I got really worried the other day because I started getting a lot of followers. It was two days of so many followers, and I was like, “Oh my God, I’ve been doxxed.” The last thing I logged was Hitch. My top four are A Woman Under the Influence, The Dark Knight, Phantom Thread, and Eternal Sunshine. My Odyssey review has 112 likes. I don’t care how many followers I have on Instagram, how many likes I get. Letterboxd is truly a hit of adrenaline every time someone likes my review. —Frazier Tharpe

I cannot shut up about this, but it has to be throwing the first pitch out for the White Sox. I will remember that forever.

Yeah, it was. And I hadn’t thrown a baseball in a really long time.

Age: 38Breakout moment: Her riveting performance as the title character in Chris Rock’s Misty Green (and her role in Slow Horses).—————

I mean, the first time I watched it, I just thought, Oh—you’re going to be in every frame. [Laughs]

[In an American accent] “Murderer.” Mur-der-rer. I think every Brit finds that hard. I was like, “Can I say ’killer’ instead?” And “mirror.” It’s when you’ve got a double r. But I had a great dialect coach.

I would’ve loved to have been in a scene with Philip Seymour Hoffman. That would’ve been my dream. And I would love to work with Sandra Hüller.

I was onstage with Ralph Fiennes. We finished Misty Green—I did an overnight shoot on Friday into Saturday morning, got on a plane, arrived in London on Sunday, and went straight into rehearsals with Ralph. It was a pretty mad summer.

I was getting ready before the Misty Green premiere [at the Toronto International Film Festival]. And my mom came down. She sat there and she was just sending pictures and she just had tears in her eyes. Just seeing this whole period at TIFF—for her, it’s her little girl. And every now and again, I would just look at her and she was just happy and in awe in a way. It warmed my heart. —A.P.

Dress by Gucci. Hosiery by Wolford. Earrings by Cartier.

Shirt and pants by Brioni. Bow tie and cummerbund, stylist’s own. Vintage watch by Rolex from Foundwell. Vintage cufflinks by Cartier from Foundwell. Earring, his own.

Age: 35Breakout moment: The Cannes hit Club Kid, which he wrote, directed, and starred in as a party promoter navigating unexpected parenthood.—————

I still do, yeah, at the phone call scene at the end. But it feels very distant from me. I feel like I’m watching it more as an observer now, which is nice, and I feel different than when I made it. I was such an open wound.

Cannes was such an emotional experience. I’m 35, and I’ve always known that I had something to say and I knew how to say it, but I’ve never been given the opportunity. So, honestly, to feel like I was being seen and understood was like 20 years of validation that I didn’t get. And so something shifted, that week, in me. I’ve noticed myself not having to use humor as a shield anymore, as much. I’m less afraid to be vulnerable—and not just in my public life but in my personal life too.

Of course, you shouldn’t need the validation of anyone to feel like you’re worthy, but let’s be real.…

What gagged me this year? I would say, honestly, as a statement of ambition, the Javis’ movie [La Bola Negra]. You marvel at it because you’re like, “How the fuck did they pull this off?” To even want to tell that complex of a story, and to just do it and go balls to the wall, is very inspiring.

I was like, “This is too long. It’s embarrassing now.” —A.P.

Jacket and pants by Canali. Shirt by Amiri. Boots by Alessandro Vasini. Tie by Husbands.

Age: 41Breakout moment: Reporting on how the Steve Ballmer–owned Clippers were allegedly funny with money, on his podcast Pablo Torre Finds Out, leading to one of the most severe team penalties in sports history.—————

Two days ago, I walked into this event that The New York Times was holding…it was like the 175th birthday party [for the paper]. I walk out the elevator and literally the first person I see is [NBA commissioner] Adam Silver. I won’t say exactly what he told me, but the tenor of our conversation was like, This is the HBO series adaptation of what this moment would be.

As a first-generation American—my parents are from the Philippines—there’s always been a dream of mine to be cast as a white person. So…Andrew Garfield, come on. Let’s do this.

The funny part is that I’m not even done reporting this story yet. The federal government is now investigating the Clippers, and so I’m now investigating the government’s investigation in the era of Trump too, which is like its own rabbit hole inside of a rabbit hole, which is fucking wild. But at the same time, it feels like now our number is listed in the phone book…actual other NBA owners are calling me up saying, “Can you look into this?”

It’s hard not to be sappy about this, but the lesson I learned is that if you’re telling the truth, you can walk into any room and feel like that as a suit of armor. Even when the person you’re investigating is one of the 10 richest people in the world. —F.T.

Coat by Michael Kors. Vintage pajamas from Palace Costume Co. Socks by London Sock Company. Glasses by Jacques Marie Mage. Necklaces (throughout), his own.

Age: 37Breakout moments: Stealing scenes as a ’70s Provincetown line cook in the Anthony Bourdain biopic Tony, and his stand-up special Uncle Stav.—————

I just did The Drew Barrymore Show. That experience was so magical. I was so nervous to be around Drew Barrymore. I’ve done a lot of cool shit, and that’s the one where I was like, “I am actually nervous to the point where I’m not a professional. I’m just a stammering little boy who’s with the girl he had a crush on from The Wedding Singer when he was a fat 10-year-old, and I’m trying to keep it together.”

Last week I allowed myself an aimlessly wander around New York City day. Got a little stoned, walked around, stumbled into a movie. Walked along the water on the West Side. Sat down to a little random omakase. Rode a Citi Bike around. I call it the hero’s journey, where you leave your home at, whatever, 11 a.m. ,with just the bare provisions, and you’re not allowed to go home. You just have to keep moving and keep doing stuff.

I filmed this Judd Apatow movie The Comeback King, with Glen Powell, Cristin Milioti, Madelyn Cline. That comes out in February. I’m excited to do stand-up—for fun, not to explicitly work on another hour. I’m excited to go back to clubs and just like, “Can I be the funniest person in this club tonight?” And that’s my only goal.

To be honest with you, the last three years, it’s [been] all go. But you got to have these big years, or else GQ doesn’t talk to you at the end of the year. —A.P.

On Halkias: Jacket and pants by Brooks Brothers. Shirt by Many. Shoes by Louis Vuitton Men’s. Sunglasses by Valention. On Torre: Jacket by Tom Ford. Shirt by Factor’s. Pants by Louis Vuitton Men’s. Shoes by G.H. Bass. Sunglasses by Jacques Marie Mage. Ring, his own.

Shirts and pants by Dunhill. Underwear by Calvin Klein. Earrings (throughout), his own.

Age: 31Breakout moment: Storming the West Coast rap scene with collabs from Pharrell Williams and Tyler, the Creator on his major-label debut album, No Rest for the Wicked.—————

Not like I made it, but I have arrived. We went to Magic City on a Monday for the first time. They were playing the fuck out my records in that motherfucker. The DJ played “Look Like My Mama” in the strip club. You ain’t never heard Tyler, the Creator get played in a strip club. We crossing worlds that’s never been crossed before.

The day before the album came out, we were in Area 29 in Houston, doing our thing. Regular night, it ain’t too packed, throwing money. They tap me on my shoulder like, “My boy want to holler at you.” They take me upstairs to this private section, pull the curtain back…it’s Travis Scott.

It was epic, bro. N-gga came out from behind the curtain like Batman. The light hid half his face. He said, “I fuck with everything you got going on.” We go back down to that section, 15 minutes pass, then one of the owners came and grabbed me: “Look, look, look.” Travis was up there, playing [Chike’s song] “Packed Up.” —F.T.

Shirt by Lu’u Dan. Pants by Valentino. Bracelet by Cartier.

Age: 26Breakout moment: Being a dead ringer for Sly Stallone in I Play Rocky, the film about…Sly Stallone playing Rocky.—————

In the thick of prep. I was training at Gleason’s Gym with Stephan James, who plays Carl Weathers. We were beating the crap out of each other and figuring out how to box. I had gained a bunch of weight, and I was trying to actually cut a few pounds, so I was in those sweatsuits, like a garbage-bag type thing. On the way to the gym, I would listen to Stallone and on the way home.… Everything I could find up to 1980, 1985, every piece of Stallone footage that existed.

I [asked] Seth Rogen, “Dude, how do you handle this stuff?” And he said, “Just go with the flow.” It was such a simple answer, but from somebody like him, I was like, He’s got a good grip on this. The simplicity of it landed.

Philip Seymour Hoffman is one. Chadwick Boseman. Christian Bale, Heath Ledger.

[The first Rocky] is the best movie—but when I was younger, the one that caught my eye was four. Rocky IV is nuts. —F.T.

Jacket by Versace. Shirt by 73London. Pants by Umit Benan. Earring (throughout), his own.

(from l.) On Ambrossi: All clothing by Gucci. Shoes by Christian Louboutin. Pocket square by Brioni. Sunglasses by Ahlem. On Calvo: Jacket, pants, and boots by Tom Ford. Scarf, stylist’s own. Sunglasses by Jacques Marie Mage.

(from l.) On Ambrossi: Jacket and pants by Chanel. Shirt by Factor’s. Shoes by Marsèll. Pin by Cartier. On Calvo: All clothing, tie, and shoes by Ralph Lauren Purple Label. Watch by Cartier.

Ages: Ambrossi, 42 | Calvo, 35Breakout moment: Cowriting and directing La Bola Negra, the gay Spanish historical epic loosely based on an unfinished Federico García Lorca play.——————————————

Javier Ambrossi: Very important. We understood that sometimes people expect LGBTQ+ stories to be niche or for a specific audience. We wanted to do a queer story about universal love.

Ambrossi: It’s a way to put in context how different we are. We change a lot, and the story of our grandparents and our story is very different.

Javier Calvo: What do we do with the freedom our ancestors fought for, and died for?

Calvo: Isabelle Huppert. For me, she’s everything.

Ambrossi: I will go with Julia Roberts. My Best Friend’s Wedding is a very important queer story. The relationship between a woman and a gay man—it was the first time I saw that onscreen. And Rupert Everett was so bold, and I could tell in his eyes that he was gay. I was a kid and I was like, “He’s not faking it.”

How did you celebrate when you won best director at Cannes?

Ambrossi: I put the award on the table and went to sleep. And then Javi came over at 8 a.m.…

Ambrossi: “Give me the award!” And he took it! —A.P.

(from l.) On Ippolito: Sunglasses by Jacques Marie Mage. Necklace by Cartier. On Chike: Shirt and pants by Brioni. Sunglasses by Tom Ford. On Thiele: Jacket by McQueen. Shirt by Factor’s. Pants by Versace.

#6679. Inside Designer Carla Fernández’s Vibrant Mexico City Home, Full of Indigenous Textiles and Art

When the Mexico City–based writer and curator Su Wu started working on her new design book, a theme began to emerge: She kept noticing the presence of pre-Hispanic architectural and design traditions in modern Mexican interiors.

It was from this realization that Mesoamerican Modern: Ancient Ideas in Contemporary Mexican Homes was born. The book features 16 homes across Mexico—those of artists, architects, designers, and more—all shot by photographer Luis Garvan.

Wrought iron doors designed by Carla Fernández.

“When I began this project, I thought that we would be documenting a history, like the aura surrounding the artifacts in a museum, the apparitions of the past,” Wu writes in the book’s introduction. “Instead, what I’ve found is the vibrant, living presence of Indigenous ideas in Mexico.”

“In the guest bedroom, pink silk curtains dyed by Fernández cast a glow in the room. The bedspread is from her personal collection of Mesoamerican textiles, made of undyed wool in Teotitlán del Valle, Oaxaca.”—Su Wu

One space that exemplifies those ideas is Casa Flora, the colorful brutalist Mérida home and studio of the Mexican fashion designer Carla Fernández. As a designer, Fernández seeks to preserve the textile traditions of Indigenous and Mestizo communities through collaborations with artisans, bringing ancient practices to a contemporary audience. Fittingly, the home is full of Mesoamerican art and textiles.

Fernández led the renovation of the house, which dates back more than a century and now hosts workshops for Taller Flora, her design lab, which focuses on traditional Indigenous clothing techniques. (You can also stay in the home; the designer lists it for stays on Airbnb.)

Fernández’s husband, the artist Pedro Reyes, created several elements of the space, including a concrete outdoor shower, as well as some of the tiles throughout the space. His sculptures—including a prototype of Mitla Sillas, chairs inspired by the pyramids of the Mitla archaeological site in Oaxaca—are found throughout the home.

The outdoor shower designed by Pedro Reyes.

Reflecting on Fernández’s home, Wu tells Vogue: “I was very moved when writing the book with the idea of the ‘void’ in Mesoamerican architecture, and how so many architects, going back to the writings of Teodoro González de León, described the patio as a central, defining feature of the Mesoamerican home, a space that is at once enclosed and open to the elements. And in Carla’s home, I think there are many moments that exemplify this idea of the boundary between interior and exterior being not a physical one, but rather a marker of movement across psychological states.”

“A latticed window, known in Mexico as a celosia, is a central part of Mesoamerican architecture, used for cross-ventilation and to create a permeable sense of separation and shadow.”—Su Wu

Prototypes of Reyes’s Mitla Sillas chairs.

For Wu, Casa Flora is also a prime example of an idea that chef Elena Reygadas wrote about in her foreword to the book, “which is that handmade things are meant to be lived with no matter how precious, or especially if precious.”

“An equipale chair made of bent wood by the Wixárika people in Nayarit sits in front of a backstrap-loom tapestry of the Torre Latinoamericana, the iconic skyscraper in Mexico City designed by architect Augusto H. Álvarez. Made by artisans from San Juan Cancuc, Chiapas, the tapestry echoes a collection of dresses from Carla Fernández that featured historic buildings in Mexico City.”—Su Wu

All images © Luis GarvanCasa Flora, Mérida, Yucatán (pages 244-263). Mesoamerican Modern: Ancient Ideas in Contemporary Mexican Homes by Su Wu. Published by Phaidon Press.

#6677. Do You Recognize These Past GQ Breakouts?

After a seven-year hiatus, we’re thrilled to announce the return of GQ’s Breakouts portfolio. First launched in 2014, the series has highlighted (often young) stars just as they hit. Our group in 2026 includes Belmont Cameli of Off Campus, Owen Thiele of Adults, and Rosalind Eleazar of Misty Green. We also expanded our scope beyond actors: There’s the electronic musician duo Fcukers, the Spanish director duo Los Javis, Pablo Torre—a longtime podcaster and journalist who won the Pulitzer Prize—and many more. We suspect you’ll be seeing them around for years.

The people we’ve featured in past breakouts have often gone on to become beloved household names. For instance, when this portfolio first began, we included Shailene Woodley, Jack O’Connell, and the late Chadwick Boseman. In 2017, we featured Timothée Chalamet, now a bona fide movie star. Jack Lowden, also included that year, is a frontrunner to play James Bond. And so on.

As we bring back this portfolio, it felt only fitting to take a trip down memory lane. Everyone, after all, has to start somewhere. Here are some stars who were once GQ Breakouts:

A mansion in LA. The best party clothes of the season. An electric musical duo. A model turned actor. A comedian turned director. At least two podcasters. Come on in and meet the 13 people who just had the biggest, most-spotlight-stealing year of their lives.

#6676. I Have No Intention of Celebrating the Blue Tsunami Until Scott Colom Is Officially Elected

And haste is now required, because the election-denying harpies are once again gathering in the lush canopies of the Intertube forests.

I would like the nation to know that I have no intention of celebrating the events of last Tuesday until Mississippi gets its damn act together and sends Scott Colom to the Senate. The man is too valuable a personality—and his election far too significant—to delay his appearance in Washington any longer. And haste is now required, because the election-denying harpies are gathering in the lush canopies of the Intertube forests again.

First in flight is Mike Lindell. Pillow Dude is not satisfied with squandering his late-night TV millions on quixotic paranoid fandangos. He promises that, next Monday, he will reveal the Real Story about how the voices in his head stole all the elections last week. The president already is on board, as if he ever really disembarked from the SS Crazytown. From Time:

Meanwhile, Lindell keeps digging. From NBC News:

Yes, Bunky. The polls were wrong. They underestimated the size of the catastrophe the president would bring down on Republicans at every level of government. This has resulted in a vigorous revival of the Floor Space School of election denial. This is the one that puts up a map of a state’s counties and then expresses amazement that the votes are heavily weighted toward those places where people actually live. Rocks and lizards do not vote, people, although several of the latter have received Cabinet positions recently.

And there seems to be some weird concentration on the city of Galveston and its environs. As best as I can understand it, the deniers point out that Ken Paxton carried Galveston County 52–46, and if Paxton carried Galveston County by six points, how in God’s name could James Talarico have won the election by 12 points?

#6675. Alex Cooper and Alix Earle’s Feud Timeline, for Everyone Highly Confused

Yes, Alix Earle and Alex Cooper are STILL feuding, and if that means nothing to you, there’s a LOT to catch up on. We’re talking nearly two years of drama and (hot) mess, so settle in for a comprehensive timeline that’ll prepare you for the next time someone in your group chat asks you to explain the situation.

Alex Cooper launches The Unwell Network and signs none other than Alix Earle. Alex says in a statement, “Alix took the U.S. by storm with her ‘get ready with me’ videos, college lifestyle, and endearing personality. I feel honored to be at a place in my career where I can pass along knowledge and advice for a new generation of creators to flourish.”

Alix’s dad, TJ Earle, hints at drama in an episode of Hot Mess, saying he’s had to have “hard conversations with people” throughout “every deal” when they don’t realize Alix’s full potential or what “she needs to be doing.”

He also mentioned an incident where he had to handle some drama for his daughter without involving her and described the incident as “a business situation, but it was also something else.”

Fans notice that Alix skipped Unwell’s Super Bowl party in New Orleans, leading to speculation of a feud...and just a few weeks later, Variety reports that Unwell has dropped Alix’s Hot Mess podcast.

Following the bombshell, a source tells Us Weekly, “They’ve had a lot of drama between them [since] they decided to collaborate on Unwell. They clashed over business, and their friendship is over because of it.”

Another insider adds that “Alix is working on bigger projects not related to the podcast space. Alix’s dad got more involved in her business decisions and did not like what Unwell was doing. He ultimately thought it ended up not being a smart business move.”

That’s when Alix hops on TikTok and says, “There’s been a lot of chatter online this week about me and work. I also have no idea what’s going on.”

Alix drops a TikTok saying she’s pausing her podcast, saying, “I have to put a pause on podcasting right now for the foreseeable future. Don’t really want to get into the details of it all, and I kind of can’t get into the details of it all right now, but I’ve loved it so, so much, and I’m really proud of what I built with the podcast.”

In response, Alex denies that she is the reason Alix is no longer doing her podcast in a TikTok, claiming that “Alix not being able to podcast has nothing to do with Unwell” and that “Unwell gave her everything back,” which means Alix owns her intellectual property.

Alix cryptically talks about her Unwell departure with The Wall Street Journal, saying, “That was, behind the scenes, a little bit of a hot mess.”

Alix kinda goes off on TikTok, saying, “My Co–Star told me that I can start shit today. I mean, is this my time that I’ve been waiting for to go? I have so much information. We could go. I wake up every day, and I’m like, ‘What should I do Co-Star?’ And today, it’s like, ‘Revenge. Let’s go get ’em.’”

She then responds to a commenter who wrote, “Yes tell us what happened with Alex Cooper thank you,” with, “How much time do you have?”

Alex uses “Circus” by Britney Spears to caption an Instagram post highlighting Unwell events in Vegas, which everyone thinks is pointed since Alix danced to the song on Dancing With the Stars. The post has since been deleted.

Alix allegedly reposts this TikTok coming for Alex, after which Alex drops a TikTok calling out Alix for being passive-aggressive with her reposts and reminding her that she never signed an NDA.

“Hey girl, the passive aggressive reposts and the likes and the commenting on things—I’ve got to call you out here,” Alex says. “You’re going to need to get specific and just say what you’ve got to say about me. There’s no NDA. No one is stopping you. Stop hiding behind other people and just say it yourself.”

She adds, “What’s the beef? ‘Cause I’m really tired of waking up and seeing you using this fake drama to distract from other shit going online from you. Not interested. I know what happened, and so do you. So talk. Unless the fake narrative that you’re creating happens to be way more interesting than the truth. I have nothing to hide when it comes to you and me. So unless you actually have something to say, I’m out, this is over, have a good Monday everyone.”

Meanwhile, David Portnoy chimes in with what he’s heard, saying Alix was “too big” when she was signed to Unwell, and that there was drama over them sharing the same PR person and a Carl’s Jr. commercial that Alix starred in.

As he profoundly put it, “Go back to Carl’s burger.”

Nearly a week after Alex’s callout, Alix takes to TikTok to give a response to Alex…kind of. She posts a video of herself practicing pole-dancing moves, set to the song “You Don’t Own Me” by SAYGRACE featuring G-Eazy.

Her caption: “Sorry been busy filming this week.”

During an appearance on the Today show, Alix is asked about the feud head-on.

“There’s been this back and forth between you and another internet personality, a podcaster as well, [and] there seems to be a bit of online beef there,” anchor Craig Melvin says. “What’s actually happening? What’s really going on?”

In response, Alix says she’s been “trying to keep things pretty positive,” but Craig isn’t to be deterred. He then doubles down, asking if there is “legitimate contentiousness” to the internet drama or if it has “all been exaggerated.”

Alix’s response: “No, it’s exaggerated. I love everyone.”

As part of her profile for 2026’s TIME100 Creators in July, Alix confirms that she will discuss her fallout with Alex on her upcoming reality show, Earle Meets World.

“It’s something I’m going to talk about on the show,” she says of the feud. “That was one of the things in the beginning they wanted me to talk about, and I really wasn’t comfortable sharing. Then everything unfolded the way it did while we were filming. There was a serious struggle behind the scenes of emotions, and my family showed up for me. Those moments that sometimes seem so fun for people online, but are hard behind the scenes—I am happy that we were able to capture that.”

At long last, Earle Meets World premieres, where Alix has a LOT to say. For starters: She says Alex “took advantage of my admiration of her” and “sold her on a dream.”

She also claims that she’s “scared of Alex Cooper” and worried about “how she’s going to twist things,” adding, “I just get scared because everyone tells me not to fight with her. Because they always win and that they always come out on top…She even said to me, ‘You don’t want to go to war with me.’ I definitely feel betrayed by Alex Cooper.”

Alix even drafts a text message to Alex at one point, in which she calls Alex “manipulative and toxic” and “full of false promises.”

Six years after falling out over contract negotiations with Barstool Sports and Dave Portnoy, Alex’s Call Her Daddy cofounder Sofia Franklyn releases a tell-all book called Daddy Issues...and Alix takes to TikTok to show her support, posting a video of herself reading Sofia’s book.

#6674. The Fall Winter 2026 Fashion Trends to Know and Shop Now

If recent seasons have been defined by understated dressing, the fall-winter 2026 trends mark a shift toward a more expressive approach to style. However, there’s still a piece of the cake for every aesthetic to cut into. Across the runways, designers embraced the art of contrast. By pairing clean, minimalist silhouettes with rich textures—and reimagining classic wardrobe staples with unexpected details like a peplum or a voluminous shape—they proved that practicality and personality can coexist. The result is a season that feels both wearable and exciting.

Leading the conversation this season are six standout trends that capture fashion’s evolving mood. The Updated Lady introduces a fresh take on suiting for the modern woman, complete with feminine touches and softened structures. (Take a look at Chanel, Balenciaga, and Jil Sander for fresh inspiration, for starters.) Elsewhere, collections at Khaite, Kallmeyer, and Conner Ives nodded to the golden age of the 1920s, incorporating elegant Art Deco touches throughout. An unexpected take on proportions has entered the chat, too, with Tory Burch and Celine reconfiguring preconceived notions of size and shape. Designers, from Prada to Loewe, leaned on the power of styling, too, showcasing how layering and a close attention to detail can transform a wardrobe. For a more alluring take on the season, Alaïa and Valentino celebrated the female form through body-hugging silhouettes and delicate sheer pieces. Meanwhile, Dries Van Noten and Erdem embraced the spirit of fall with down-to-earth palettes of green and brown, punctuated by dark floral patterns.

Discover a shoppable first look at the key fall winter 2026 fashion trends before they fully take over. Don’t forget to come back to this story for more.

Unlike past seasons, there wasn’t one definitive silhouette for tailoring this fall. Let’s start with suiting. Designers don’t want you to stay within the lines. Many jackets were shown with mismatched trousers and skirts; lapel linings were exposed; traditional collared shirts were left undone and wrinkled; collars were turned up and styled with one in and one out. Some blazers shrank; some went long and lean. Others were cropped, nipped in, or even made sculptural.

In autumn’s sea of black coats and navy sweaters, choose color. This season, designers made it simple, showing a slew of head-to-toe looks in vivid monochrome hues. Take your pick! The runways offered a spin around the contemporary color wheel with splashes of color-drenched leather bags and footwear. From knits to denim, dresses to outerwear, we can’t think of a reason not to try this kind of energetic dressing.

This isn’t your grandmother’s lace! On the fall runways, designers from Altuzarra and Colleen Allen to Bottega Veneta, Dior, and Gabriela Hearst embraced a more dramatic kind of romance, with cascading layers, frothy ruffles, and sheer, sweeping silhouettes. Get the look with a delicate lace blouse paired with a tiered black skirt, or go all in with a floor-length lace dress. The effect is romantic, but anything but prim.

Photos: Courtesy of Kallmeyer, Khaite, Rabanne, Chanel, and Loro Piana

Although F. Scott Fitzgerald’s 1925 novel, The Great Gatsby, predates the height of the design style, its themes of 1920s luxury have permanently associated the story with the movement’s signature geometric patterns and overt opulence. From Khaite to Colleen Allen, designers embraced the era with richly detailed texture, glamorous shapes, fringe, gloves, and velvet. To quote the iconic host himself: “Can’t repeat the past? Why, of course, you can!” For 2026, the era returns with a modern, streamlined touch—ready for daily wear to elegant soirées.

Photos: Courtesy of Chanel, Stella McCartney, Givenchy, Jil Sander, and Chloé

The fall-winter 2026 runways showcased a sense of whimsy through bold proportions, featuring oversized designs, cropped styles, and exaggerated silhouettes. Cocoon-like tailoring, dramatic hemlines, and billowy pants and dresses create an expressive and eye-catching approach to fashion. This trend embraces a bit of drama (because that’s life!) while also maintaining a sense of balance. Unconventional silhouettes might feel daunting to incorporate into your daily lineup, so start with small touches. Destree’s jacquard blouse balances voluminous shoulders with a fitted waist that pairs easily with jeans, while Aflalo’s tapered white balloon pants feel airy and light—just add a layered knit and coat for transitional dressing.

Photos: Courtesy of Ferragamo, Loewe, Prada, Johanna Parv, The Row (middle)

Primarily inspired by the fall/winter 2026 Prada runway, the power of styling was a reminder that great style isn’t about owning more—it’s about seeing familiar pieces, like cardigans and tailored trousers, in a new light. Everyday wardrobe staples were transformed through unique pairings, layered basics, and unexpected accessories to create ensembles that demand a second glance. Styling, too, becomes a vessel for self-expression, allowing you to make each piece your own through personal touches and a careful attention to detail.

Photos: Courtesy of Dior, Chanel, Givenchy, Balenciaga, Proenza Schouler, Kallmeyer

Current, confident, and sophisticated, the Updated Lady emerged as one of fall-winter 2026’s defining archetypes, with houses like Chanel, Givenchy, and Balenciaga reimagining power dressing through a contemporary lens. Rather than rigid corporate suiting, the season favored softened structures, explored through gently sloping waists, sculpted jackets, and rich textures that balance strength with ease. The modern woman is multifaceted and full of contradictions, balancing polish with her own distinct edge—shouldn’t her wardrobe follow suit?

Photos: Courtesy of Gucci, Gorunway.com, Khaite and Alaïa

Bodies, bodies, bodies! The fall 2026 runways were an homage to the female form. From the second-skin dresses at Alaïa—some of which actually walked in flesh-colored nude—to Khaite’s sheer layers and Valentino’s lingerie-inspired slips, little was left to the imagination. Translating the trend into your own fall rotation may seem far-fetched, but by embracing it piece by piece, you can achieve an alluring look without going full naked dressing. Imagine Jil Sander’s knit bustier top with a slim trouser, Gucci’s cut-out leggings grounded by a crisp white tee, or one of Saint Lauren’s lace mini dresses beneath a sharp tailored blazer. To bring it back to one of the season’s strongest show notes—and one we outlined above: It all comes down to the styling.

It’s not unusual for fall trends to find inspiration in the countryside—whether it’s heritage plaids, khaki barn jackets, or some kind of cozy knit variation. As grounding as the source material may be, this season’s expression is more elaborate than ever. Nowhere is that more apparent than in the floral prints peppered throughout the collections: they’re richer, deeper, and even moodier than in years’ past. Par for the course, intricate floral brocades appeared at Dries Van Noten on everything from outerwear to midiskirts. Simone Rocha and Erdem also showcased their fair share of flora and fauna, from draped dresses to blouses, proving that the look can move between moods and occasions.

#6673. Abdul El-Sayed Is a ‘Retinol King’

Experts review the senator-elect’s skin care routine.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Now that the midterms are over and we’ve seen a blue wave, the way people are talking about the candidates who won has shifted. Instead of policy, we’ve been able to focus on other issues: namely, how hot many of the candidates are (a welcome change). My social media feeds have been littered with photos of newly elected politicians like James Talarico, Jon Ossoff, Rob Sand, and Abdul El-Sayed. And while Sand may have modeled for Prada back in the day, El-Sayed’s complexion is near-perfect. As it turns out, the doctor-turned-politician has a killer skin care routine.

In a video from May that resurfaced and went viral on X last week, El-Sayed revealed his skin care regimen in a video with comedian Suzanne Lambert. Though at first El-Sayed tried to pass off his glowing complexion as “good genetics,” he has a full routine to help. The senator-elect begins his day with CeraVe Facial Cleanser. After that, he uses EltaMD AOX UV Elements SPF 50 sunscreen, which he reapplies halfway through the day. At night, he washes his face with the same CeraVe cleanser. Every other day, he uses Differin, a retinoid, to increase cell turnover. El-Sayed is a fan of the “sandwich method,” where you use moisturizer both before and after retinol (he opts for EltaMD PM Restore Moisturizer). It’s clear that El-Sayed has done his homework (he is a doctor, after all). Below, I asked three skin experts to give their thoughts on his skin care routine.

“Okay, I’m obsessed. A retinol king with a strong SPF commitment—extremely impressed. The only thing missing in my opinion is a low and slow daily exfoliation to help shift some of that cell buildup that can occur with retinol use. I’m of course going to recommend Sofie Pavitt Face Mandelic Clearing Serum. If he’s ever in New York City I’d love for him to come in for a facial with me, my treat.”

“Of course he has an evidence-backed routine. He’s using a cleanser in the morning and then a sunscreen which can double as a moisturizer (although the EltaMD one is not super moisturizing). Reapplying that around 12 P.M. or 1 P.M. is fantastic advice. The one thing that he is wrong on—and I hate that I’m using that term—is that adapalene is not tretinoin. He keeps calling adapalene retinoic acid, and they are not the same thing. They are both vitamin A derivatives, approved for drug use, but adapalene is approved for acne, while tretinoin is approved for acne and antiaging. They’re same-same but different. Adapalene is great for acne, but if you’re worried about acne and aging, you’re probably going to be using retinoic acid. Other than that, my man, he’s got it. He’s gold.” [Editor’s note: you could also try the Dieux Firm Revival HydroRetinal Firming Serum.]

“Starting with a CeraVe face wash? A bipartisan choice if I’ve ever seen one. It’s gentle, effective, accessible, and works for a wide range of skin types and ages. Honestly, one of the few things I think we can get both sides of the aisle to agree on. Following that up with SPF that he actually reapplies? Now that’s the kind of commitment to prevention I can get behind. A politician who understands the value of protecting against future damage? How refreshing. But where Senator Abdul really earns my attention is when he casually drops the retinol sandwich method into the conversation. It’s an excellent strategy for people whose skin doesn’t tolerate retinol directly. I have one friendly amendment to the senator’s skin care legislation: If your skin can tolerate it, consider applying retinol directly to clean, dry skin before moisturizing. That can allow for more direct delivery of the active ingredient, although the sandwich method can still be effective.”

#6672. “Dance-Offs” Are Coming to ‘Dancing With the Stars’ Tonight: Here’s How the Scoring Works

It’s Grammy Night on Dancing With the Stars, and there’s a twist! On top of performing to award-winning songs, the remaining couples (minus Harry Shum Jr. and Jenna Johnson) are competing in “dance-offs,” where they’ll be performing the same dance style to the same song—at the same time. A little confused as to how the whole thing goes down? Here’s what to expect tonight.

As the show puts it in their official press release: “Two at a time, couples face off against one another on the ballroom floor as they dance head-to-head in the same style at the same time. Winners of each of the three Dance-Offs will be selected by the judges, and the winning couple from each dance-off will receive bonus points added to their judges’ totals for the night.”

Here are the pairings, the songs, and the dance styles:

These two have immunity thanks to getting the highest scores last week! Which means judges will automatically add the dance-off bonus points to their score this week.
