#6671. All the Gifts GQ Editors Want This Holiday Season 2026

As the desk at GQ that handles all things shopping, the GQ Recommends team has developed a rather motley assortment of wants, needs, and highly specific desires. We have good taste, if I may say as much, and no shortage of opinions about what makes a thing worth owning. So this holiday season, I asked everyone to set aside the usual assignments and show me what's on their own wishlists. What follows is an eclectic grab bag of A1 gifts, straight from the folks who spend all year finding the good stuff.

Still got names to cross off? Peruse our gift guides and sign up for the GQ Recommends newsletter for more good stuff worth giving (or keeping).

Years after getting married, I can say with confidence that the best thing about marriage is my wife. But a close second is the set of Sabre Bistrot flatware we received off our registry. A pleasure to hold and behold. And now they make chopsticks, which is unfortunate news for someone afflicted with my particular strain of completism. I have no choice but to finish the set. —MN

I couldn’t in good conscience ask for everything I want from Camiel Fortgens, which is, uh, everything. But this heavy-knit beanie, with the melange yarn and signature raw edges, feels like a reasonable place to start. —MN

It's true, the best radio station on the internet deserves its own hardware. Made in collaboration with tech company Atonemo, this dedicated player has big, tactile buttons for tuning into NTS 1 and 2, a dial for wandering through the Infinite Mixtapes, and the ability to plug straight into my amp. I feel so alive, and I've never been more excited to turn a dial. —MN

Nosying around other people’s bookshelves is a favorite pastime, and I can think of only a few libraries I’d rather rifle through than Luis Barragan’s. I’ve made the pilgrimage to his home and studio in Mexico City, and this new book offers a new look at the peerless architect’s life and influences through the personal collection he kept on his shelves. —MN

You may already know that we love Lady White’s top-tier white T-shirts, but we’re also massive fans of their sweats, especially this superweight hoodie. And when they say superweight, they mean it. I’ve own the zip-up version, and it's basically a winter coat. This will become my dog-walking hoodie, my 6 A.M. stroll to the bodega hoodie, and the late-night walk back from the cinema hoodie. —JB

Back in high school, some homies and I thought of ourselves as future producers. The music was terrible and thankfully is lost in the ether somewhere on SoundCloud, but despite those early attempts, I still find sampling a fascinating part of music. It’s a way to shut off the brain and think about songs in a new way. I doubt the music will be any better than it was back in the day, but I’ll have fun acting like Jamie XX or Nicolas Jaar, at least. —JB

I’m a pen-and-paper guy. You will often catch me feverishly jotting down thoughts in the middle of a meeting. I’ve recently needed to retire my old Moleskine notebook and found myself doing a deep dive into reusable leather-bound book holders that you can treat like a modular journal. You can add in different types of notebooks, calendars, and daily planners—the possibilities are endless. —JB

There comes a time in every man's life when he fancies himself a barista and thinks he should dedicate some time to learning how to pull a damn good espresso. I am that man, and the delusions are more errant than you would expect. Because I drink so much coffee, I’ve convinced myself I might as well get good at making it. —JB

As much as I don’t want to admit that I overindex on outerwear, my closet tells the truth. It’s so full that I can’t even shift any of the hangers without taking one of them out first. But that won’t stop me from eyeing this gorgeous coat from Auralee. It’s everything I want in a coat: dramatically long, artfully faded, and a deep black colorway. Now I’ll just have to find a new coat rack. —GO

I saw this bad boy in a shop and was so drawn to it. The colors are just right, the size is great, and the material is ultra-sturdy, but on this day, the devil on my shoulder told me not to get it that day. I came to my senses and came back the next day only to find that the tote was sold out. Hence why I’ve got it on my wish list because I believe in second chances and miracles. —GO

This little thing is a clever ear warmer that can be worn as is or can be slipped over the bill of your favorite hat. It’s silly and weird and cool and totally unnecessary. And yet, I would not be mad if this made its way into my stocking. —GO

I’ve always wanted a true Neapolitan-style jacket and getting to try on some of Natalino’s pieces in person during a pop-up in NYC has only stoked the fire. From the hand-stitched entails to the soft shoulders to the absolutely supreme fit, there’s no better jacket in my mind. —GO

No, I am not a ne'er-do-well Italian playboy loath to step into a leadership position at my dad's sprawling imports company. Yes, I would like to dress like one. —AG

Leave it to the vintage fiends in Japan to turn up a version of JCPenney’s canonical Big Mac flannel—the greatest flannel ever made, FYI—that might actually be worth the asking price. —AG

Okay, okay, okay. Once the sticker shock has worn off, think about how good of a boy I’ve been this year… —AG

You could drop $100K on leg-lengthening surgery and then rebuild your jeans rotation from scratch. Or you could just buy La Botte Gardiane’s naturally height-enhancing boots, which look great with the jeans you already own. —AG

I used to question whether the Eadem hype was another fad born of bloated influencer budgets, but dude. This balm is legit. I want every single color. You should get this for your girl for the holidays. If you’re scared of picking colors for her, opt for “Butter Mochi” (clear! unscented!) or “Sakura Shaved Ice,” a light pink that complements nearly every lip combo under the sun. —MH

I was treating my guy to his first ever foray into Buck Mason when I clocked a store employee wearing the Roper Jean and immediately lost the plot RE: which T-shirt the boy was trying on next. The silhouette! The wash! The contrast stitching! The complimentary Buck Mason tailoring for my stubby little legs! A girl can dream. A girl is still dreaming. Someone please gift this to me. —MH

Confessions of an adult seasonal allergies sufferer: I open the windows to air out my apartment despite knowing that that’s just welcoming the allergens into my home. Practical people might say I should address the issue at the root, but where’s the whimsy in that? Wouldn’t capitalism prefer that I purchase a solution to my problem instead? —MH

#6668. In ‘Possible Love,’ Jeon Do-yeon Delivers the Year’s Most Shattering Performance. Can It Take Her to the Oscars?

In the nearly three decades since she made her big-screen debut in 1997’s The Contact, Jeon Do-yeon has earned a richly deserved reputation in South Korea—and far beyond it—as the kind of actor who inhabits her characters so fully that it ceases to feel like acting at all. (International audiences likely know her best for 2007’s Secret Sunshine, a wrenching portrait of a grieving mother that made her the first Korean performer to win an acting prize at the Cannes Film Festival.)

Critics often reach for the word “fearless” to describe her, but when we speak via Zoom at the tail end of a marathon press day in Seoul, Jeon waves that notion away.

“I’m always scared, and I’m full of fear, especially in front of the camera,” she says, with a laugh. “I’ve been working as an actor for quite some time, but it’s an ongoing process.” In fact, it’s important to her that she never gets too comfortable. “I actually enjoy having that fear and anxiety, and trying my best to work through it, because sometimes something completely new comes out of that struggle.”

That willingness to sit in discomfort is vividly, devastatingly evident in Possible Love, out now on Netflix after a theatrical run in October—and recently selected as South Korea’s official submission for the 2027 Academy Awards. The film marks the long-awaited return of legendary auteur Lee Chang-dong (arguably best known for the hypnotic 2018 masterpiece Burning, which became the first Korean movie ever shortlisted for the best international feature Oscar and topped critics’ year-end lists across the West) after an eight-year hiatus, and a reunion for Jeon and Lee nearly 20 years after Secret Sunshine.

Constructed with Lee’s signature novelistic sweep, Possible Love centers on the complicated dynamic between two married couples from starkly different strata of contemporary Korean society. Jeon stars as Mi-ok, a pragmatic mask-factory worker living quietly with her young son and her husband, Ho-seok (played by her frequent screen partner Sul Kyung-gu). Years after a mass factory layoff and a violently suppressed labor strike, Ho-seok remains haunted by the trauma. When the funeral of a former co-worker brings them into the orbit of Ye-ji (Cho Yeo-jeong, best known as the rich mom in Parasite), an intellectual documentary filmmaker chronicling laid-off laborers, and her wealthy, enigmatic husband Sang-woo (Zo In-sung), Mi-ok agrees to let Ye-ji film their daily lives. Yet what begins as a study of liberal empathy—and a moving portrait of across-the-divide solidarity—gradually warps into something far more strange and ambiguous, exploring the fault lines of privilege, hidden desire, and voyeurism.

Oscar buzz has swirled around her performance—a stunning and powerfully subtle tightrope walk of stoicism and simmering longing—since the film premiered at the Venice Film Festival in September, but Jeon remains pragmatic about what all this fanfare actually means. “When I won the award at Cannes 20 years ago, I remember thinking, Is this going to change my life? Probably not. Am I going to change as an actor because I have a Cannes award?” she reflects. “I’m very happy that we’re competing at the Oscars, and it’s wonderful that people are talking about the film, but I don’t think it will change me or the way I perform in any way.”

Below, Jeon discusses her on-screen reunion with Sul Kyung-gu, the unlikely magic fostered by Lee Chang-dong’s aversion to having his actors rehearse, and how a story of trauma and class tension deeply rooted in Korean society found universal resonance.

Vogue: Now that Possible Love is streaming on Netflix and reaching a massive global audience, how are you feeling? Have you been hearing much feedback?

Jeon Do-yeon: I watched the movie once again as soon as it was on Netflix. I think Possible Love is a type of movie that leaves a different afterglow for different people, and people can analyze it differently and interpret it in endless ways. Now that it’s on Netflix, I feel like people are finding their own ways to think about the movie—it leaves a lot of food for thought. I’m very proud that people are talking a lot about it.

To go back to the beginning, you obviously have a deep history with director Lee Chang-dong. How did he first approach you about Possible Love? I believe this idea had been gestating for almost a decade.

I do know that Director Lee worked on this project for quite a long time—he had it in his mind for more than 10 years. Ten years ago, he wanted to talk about this worker and his wife, the couple who had been laid off. Sometimes that story diverged into different paths, and sometimes they were scrapped; I know there were a lot of different versions along the way. When I knew that he finally had a script, I really wanted to know what it was about. I was waiting for that script. [Laughs.]

Given how layered the story is, what struck you most when you finally read it? What drew you to Mi-ok?

When I first read the script, I could feel the 10 years’ worth of thought and effort that Director Lee put into it. I was very touched reading it, and I was like, “Of course this is a Chang-dong film. It is this good.” And then I thought about what I could relate to about my character, Mi-ok. Of all the traits that she has, I think she holds this anxiety, and that anxiety stems from her wish to protect certain things. Although Mi-ok may seem a little bit fragile or weak from the outside, I think she’s a very strong woman inside, so that is something that I took note of.

Zo In-sung as Sang-woo, Jeon Do-yeon as Mi-ok, and director Lee Chang-dong on the set of Possible Love.

One of the aspects of your performance I found most striking is the physicality—the way Mi-ok holds herself is such an important part of how her story is told. Did you develop that beforehand, or did you find it on set?

If I hadn’t worked with Director Lee before, then I would have spent a lot of time trying to learn more about this character and to deeply understand her. But I know how he works, and he always stresses emptying my mind as an actor. He wants me not to express, but to feel the character. The preparation process for me was mostly about making sure that I get very accustomed to the lines that my character speaks, so it really feels like it’s me speaking, but it’s actually Mi-ok speaking through me.

You’ve worked with Sul Kyung-gu more than any other actor in your career, I believe. So much of the film relies on the subtleties of that relationship—did your real-life friendship and trust help?

I think I really know him well in real life, and as an actor as well. But while we were working on this project, and as he transformed into the character of Ho-seok, I discovered new facets of him. There’s always a new side of him that would surprise me, so I’m still looking forward to working with him on another project so I can discover yet another new side of him. We have this friendship and trust that we’ve built over the years, and I’m pretty sure that they helped. When you’re trying to express the chemistry of a very long relationship of a married couple, sometimes you have to make efforts to show that aspect. But we didn’t have to try too hard because we already had that trust built between us. If we do look like a real married couple in the movie, I think it’s partly thanks to that.

Jeon Do-yeon as Mi-ok and Sul Kyung-gu as Ho-seok.

At the opposite end of the spectrum, this was your first time working with Cho Yeo-jeong. The dynamic between Mi-ok and Ye-ji walks a very delicate line between sisterhood and something more transactional. Did the two of you discuss that, or did you prefer to discover it in the room?

We actually didn’t talk about our characters before going into production. When you work with Director Lee, he’s sort of against the actors having too many biases or prejudices or information about characters before going into the shoot, because he believes something very novel and fresh can come out when it’s your first time being in that scene, when it’s your first time talking. I didn’t want to jinx that by speaking with her about our characters beforehand. In the beginning, my character, Mi-ok, believes that if Ye-ji films us, then that is a way of protecting my family—a way of finding hope. But as they get closer, she realizes that there is a gap between the two people, herself and Ye-ji, and the wall grows higher and higher as they get closer.

Director Lee is known for his naturalism. Did that mean doing dozens of takes until you lost the sense that you’re acting? Did scenes ever head in unexpected directions based on how you were all interacting?

Director Lee is not actually the type who shoots a lot of takes, because he doesn’t really tell us exactly what it is that he wants from a scene. What he tries to do instead is to look into the chemistry and the conversation between the actors and try to find something new from it. Instead of Director Lee saying, “Okay, let’s do the lines,” he would just say, “Why don’t we talk? Let’s talk.” He likes everything very lively and raw, so that’s how we did it, and he didn’t want us to have a lot of rehearsals. And regarding whether the direction of a scene actually changed after working—it didn’t happen, because there was no fixed direction in the beginning.

Jeon Do-yeon as Mi-ok and Cho Yeo-jeong as Ye-ji.

Korean cinema has obviously exploded in popularity globally over the past decade, and Possible Love is getting a huge amount of international attention. How do you view this global wave? Do you think much about reaching Western audiences?

I don’t have plans on “going global” or anything as an actor! But I do hope that Korean content and Korean cinema will be beloved around the globe going forward as well. It’s just such a joy to be able to share different stories and different emotions with people that speak different languages and have different cultures. It’s such a good opportunity for us to transcend borders, transcend language. I hope more of those opportunities will come.

Possible Love is rooted in a very specific Korean tragedy, yet the hollowing out of industrial working-class communities is something people recognize all over the world. Did you worry at all during filming about whether such a locally specific story would translate, especially on a global platform like Netflix?

When we were filming, we actually did think about how this is a very Korea-specific sort of incident. Would people around the world be able to resonate with this story? Particularly because there is no dramatic event that happens throughout the movie; it’s just about the four people and their emotions, and that’s what really brings a narrative towards the end. We were a little bit worried. But I actually realized after screening overseas that the issue of laid-off workers is not something that’s specific to Korea—it’s happening all around the world, and it is still happening in some parts of the world. I could feel that a lot of people who are not Korean, who don’t live in Korea, are resonating with the themes and the emotions that are captured in the movie. And the moon at the very end of the movie? Anybody in any part of the world can see the moon. That’s something that’s very universal.

This interview has been condensed and edited for clarity.

#6667. James Gunn’s DC Universe Was Weird, Ambitious, and Ultimately Unfinished

The writer-director is a casualty of Skydance’s acquisition of Warner Bros. He was building to a future that fans will now likely never see, for better or worse.

The hierarchy of power at DC Studios is changing again. On November 9, news broke that writer-director James Gunn and his producing partner Peter Safran left—later reported as ousted from—their jobs as co-CEOs and co-chairmen of DC Studios. Jeremy Latcham, a former Marvel Studios producer whose past credits include The Avengers and Gunn’s own Guardians of the Galaxy, will replace them on December 1.

Suddenly, DC fans woke up on a Monday to a new regime. But how will we look back on the four years that a prolific genre writer and director, who came up under Lloyd Kaufman’s B-movie studio Troma, oversaw an entire superhero universe?

It was a time of a lot of promise, not all of it fulfilled.

Before we start the eulogies for the DC Universe, it’s important to note that things aren’t over. Yet. Latcham will take over Gunn and Safran’s posts, so DC Studios is still up and running with more movies in the pipeline, many of which bear Gunn’s fingerprints. Man of Tomorrow, for example. The 2027 movie is a direct sequel to Superman, also written and directed by Gunn. Among its biggest moves is introducing a new Wonder Woman (rumored, but not confirmed, to be Adria Arjona). The Batman: Part II, the long-awaited sequel to Matt Reeves’s The Batman, will also open in 2028 with Gunn and Safran involved as producers. Gunn also said on Instagram they have been “engaged on another DC project which I’m writing.”

Both Gunn and Safran are expected to stay at DC, with Deadline confirming they will “continue to produce on various DC projects.” That said, Matthew Belloni at Puck published a story citing sources who say the DCU will reboot, again, after Man of Tomorrow. And at The Hollywood Reporter, Borys Kit and Aaron Crouch detailed the friction Gunn faced while at the studio, and how Gunn and Safran’s exits were forced by new Skydance leaders David Ellison and Ynon Kreiz. THR reports the DC heads felt secure after the big Warner-Paramount merger; Clayface had grossed $100 million, and Gunn was preparing a draft for the third Superman movie when they were told they were out. You can say the DCU is in a crisis right now.

For now, we can say that a critical chapter of DC is over. Gunn will no longer apply his vision and filter across all DC projects, which he must feel a little relieved about. By his own admission, which he wrote on Instagram on November 9, Gunn expressed feeling stretched thin, having to exert energy on movies and shows he wasn’t emotionally invested in to begin with. Said Gunn: “[T]he thing my ADHD hyperfocus has had the most difficulty with has been dividing my time between projects I’m incredibly passionate about and the ones I’m not so much and all the things I have to do as a studio head.”

Gunn and Safran got their jobs in late 2022, but it wasn’t until 2024 that people saw the first glimpse of their reign. Though Gunn had already worked for DC on 2021’s The Suicide Squad and created the spin-off Peacemaker, those projects were separate from other things at DC, then run by Walter Hamada. At the time, its biggest bet was Black Adam, Dwayne Johnson’s long-in-development antihero project that fell flat and led to nowhere. When Gunn took the reins of DC soon after, the former Marvel director had carte blanche to tailor the sandbox to his vision.

In January 2023, Gunn revealed his plans for DC Studios in something he called “Chapter 1: Gods and Monsters.” In a six-minute YouTube video, Gunn ran down his plans for the movies and shows that made up his master plan for the next decade of DC Studios. But for every mention of this movie or that HBO Max show, the first project carried significant weight. It was the first step, the real first impression, as a showcase of what Gunn would do with a DCU bent to his will. What icons would make it to the screen? What legend would get their spotlight? What iconic storylines might be retold on the big screen? What, really, will James Gunn’s DC become?

His first order of business was... Creature Commandos.

In fairness, Creature Commandos was a great show (if you saw it). It was an adult animated series where horror and pulp-inspired superheroes teamed up as a black ops strike force. While a show like that wouldn’t be anyone’s idea of a great introduction, Gunn’s background as the writer of movies like Tromeo & Juliet and his success with Guardians of the Galaxy for Marvel at least made sense. It basically said without ambiguity that this new DCU is being molded by no one else but Gunn.

Then, Superman premiered in summer 2025. Initially written by Gunn, the DC Studios head eventually named himself the director of DC’s first theatrical release under his stewardship. Despite my own misgivings about the movie, it was the reorientation a refreshed superhero universe needed. You simply can’t have a DC Universe without the Man of Steel, whose first appearance in Action Comics #1 in 1938 kicked off the modern superhero as we know it.

Superman made a solid first impression for DC at the cineplex. So there was plenty of optimism for the 2026 spin-off Supergirl starring Milly Alcock. Largely based on the acclaimed Tom King and Bilquis Evely graphic novel Supergirl: Woman of Tomorrow, the pseudo-sequel was poised to keep up DC’s newfound momentum. It didn’t. Supergirl hit a wall, much like Black Adam. No superhero universe is immune to duds, but given it was just DC’s second time at bat, a blemish on what still felt like a new, fresh-out-of-the-box DCU felt grave.

But one dud wasn’t enough to kneecap the DCU. The same summer saw the premiere of Lanterns, which fostered a faithful audience on HBO Sunday nights during its buzzy eight-episode first season. Despite mixed reviews, Clayface has grossed a profitable $100 million against a slim budget. Between the next Superman and Batman sequels, there was still plenty to look forward to. Until now.

James Gunn’s reign wasn’t impervious to bad news either. Since his January 2023 reveal, many of the projects have wound up on the back burner: The Authority, which had been a “passion project” for Gunn (with plenty of setup for it in Superman); Waller, a TV show with Viola Davis reprising her role from the Suicide Squad films; Paradise Lost, a political epic set in Wonder Woman’s home; and Booster Gold, a superhero comedy about a goofball from the future. And throughout his reign, Gunn put up with questions about his Batman (not Matt Reeves’s), which he would have introduced in The Brave and the Bold. Gunn sweated over the character so much that a cameo of Batman in Creature Commandos was shrouded in shadow, because he said he was still deciding on what the costume was going to look like.

Since the start of Gunn’s DC, it was clear his architecture came from a place of affection for the brand, and for very specific corners of it. His body of work reflected his scraggly upbringing as a punk creative, with empathy for characters who were too weird for their own Saturday morning cartoon. The Suicide Squad elevated such figures, like Weasel and Polka-Dot Man, whose climax involved a fight with Starro, a colossal evil starfish. Though his Superman was a sunny throwback to the Christopher Reeve classics, it was also jam-packed with the likes of Green Lantern, Mister Terrific, Metamorpho, and a villain Gunn was clearly setting up to be Bizarro.

Multiple times on social media, Gunn shared artwork from one DC book in particular: Kingdom Come, written by Mark Waid and illustrated by Alex Ross. Published in 1996, the story was a treatise of sorts by Waid, who lamented the state of superhero comics at the time. The story sees an older Superman who comes out of retirement to reform the Justice League, only to meet physical and philosophical resistance—predominantly from Batman.

Gunn’s allusions to Kingdom Come, including the exact shape of Superman’s “S” in his film, suggested he was laying the groundwork for a Kingdom Come-style event movie. It would have been an ambitious endeavor, oddly fitting to Gunn’s sensibilities. In the few years we saw Gunn at work for DC, he was building a universe where tried-and-true icons of superhero comics brushed up against the prickly and the bizarre. Where weirdness leaked from the orifices of a summer tentpole with an action figure set.

It often feels like Gunn was just getting started, but four years with two (soon three) movies written and directed by him, several TV shows—and Supergirl and Clayface, whatever you think of them—leave a big enough impression to suggest that Gunn’s DC had no shortage of imagination, even if his signature flourishes had grown familiar. Not every superhero film needs to have shock F-bombs and action scenes set to ironic licensed music, but Gunn had a lane. He excelled at staying in it.

It’s doubtful Gunn and Safran (who took on the business end of things) will stay on at DC under Skydance leadership for long. They will probably see Man of Tomorrow and The Batman: Part II through. If those movies do well, they might stick around to keep making movies, because for the most part, they have a magic that works. If the new movies fail to take off, then Gunn might finally find new stories to tell. It’s hard to say which path is the better one.

Gunn’s Kingdom Come, whatever it might have been, pointed to a future that never became a promise. In his farewell message, Gunn wrote that “the characters we set up will live on.” Whether the next era will let the DCU stay weird is another matter.

#6666. RIP to LIV, the Golf League No One Ever Really Cared About

The most remarkable thing about the era of LIV Golf, a five-year run characterized by near-parodic blunders and missteps, is that leadership and its public-facing talent don’t appear to have learned any lessons from their many failures.

From the beginning, the upstart league, established in 2021 as a rival to the dominant PGA Tour, was plagued by its own ineptitude. It’s only fitting—after LIV’s newfound lifeline, private investment firm BC Partners, reneged on a $300 million investment—that the brand’s figureheads are now celebrating the end by taking a victory lap. Tone-deaf doesn’t begin to describe it.

LIV CEO Scott O’Neil—who took over for original boss and Major winner Greg Norman in January of 2025 and was faced with finding new cash flow after Saudi Arabia's Public Investment Fund announced in April that it would no longer finance the operation—clearly exists in a different reality than our own. “Together, our team delivered world-class individual and team competitions across five continents, built a global business, secured pathways to The Open and U.S. Open… and built a league capable of attracting both elite and rising talent from around the world,” he said in a statement late last week. It’s unclear if the farewell tour will continue, though, an even newer report suggests that BC is looking to purchase a bankruptcy loan from LIV’s current owners. If this Hail Mary works, LIV’s future would look much different than the one O’Neil has promised.

Another way to look at it? One of LIV’s biggest accomplishments was that the league, despite its intentions to diversify the game and bring fun back into a sport that had come to personify privilege and exclusivity, inadvertently consolidated the PGA Tour’s power as a monopolistic behemoth now primed to solidify its status as the only league in town. They helped usher in a new era of leadership and investment for the Tour, including the introduction of CEO Brian Rolapp, who is by all accounts the most well-liked and accomplished boss it’s had in quite some time.

LIV also managed to tarnish the reputations of its biggest stars. Jon Rahm, who was vehemently opposed to the dog and pony show when it was announced, only to sheepishly take a bag of blood money so big his great grandchildren will never have to work a day in their lives, is now one of the game’s great “What If?” stories. Prior to the US Open at Brookline Country Club in June 2022, the defending champion was asked about his thoughts on the upstart league. “Money is great,” he said. But Rahm and his wife thought about it further. “Will our lifestyle change if I got $400 million? No, it will not change one bit.” Just over a year later, he defected to LIV.

Then there’s Phil Mickelson, who was poised to become a fixture of the American Ryder Cup team and widely considered a shoo-in to hold a cushy job as an analyst for golf’s biggest broadcasting events. Upon joining the league, he admitted in a conversation with Alan Shipnuck that Saudi Arabia was a terrible bedfellow. (Shipnuck and Mickelson have disagreed over whether the comments were on or off the record.) “They killed Khashoggi and have a horrible record on human rights. They execute people over there for being gay,” he explained. But there was a caveat: LIV was simply a “once-in-a-lifetime opportunity to reshape how the PGA Tour operates.” Miraculously, he got that part right.

Prize money on the PGA Tour is greater than ever, the Tour’s main events have recalibrated, and its season-ending championship series has been completely reimagined. Mickelson won’t be allowed within 100 miles of it.

The league’s biggest attraction, Bryson DeChambeau, is theoretically the worst-situated of the game’s many turncoats, though he’s probably more excited about growing his YouTube channel than he is about playing golf anyway. No one’s put their reputation on the line for this gaggle of buffoons quite like DeChambeau, who spoke proudly about how LIV was “growing the game” and “bringing team golf to the world.” This past weekend, he shared a statement on the dissolution of LIV. “I still believe in team golf,” he wrote. “It may look different in the future, but I still believe it’s essential to growing the game.” A loyal foot soldier, even from the grave.

It was always about the money. Nothing more, nothing less. This was a cash grab of epic proportions, and golfers not good enough to earn their keep on the PGA Tour took a life raft and briefly found themselves in possession of luxury superyachts. Then, low tide left them holding bags of cash and not much else. Was it worth it? For most of these golfers, probably. They were past their primes when LIV began; they’re still past the primes now, and just a helluva lot richer.

The league positioned itself as a disruptor, and disrupted so poorly that they’ve turned the PGA Tour into the strongest version of itself we’ve seen since Tiger Woods was at his peak. All that bloviating about the good of LIV Golf from its loudest talking heads is a bunch of nonsense.

It’s hard to tell if these statements are one last line of defense or more genuine delusion from LIV’s last advocates. They came in with the idea of sticking it to the PGA Tour, which at the time was facing bleak circumstances, bogged down in nonsense before redirecting its energy towards batting this gnat into extinction. Ultimately, LIV helped the PGA Tour save itself.

Now it’s gone for good, and five billion dollars later, we’re not gonna remember a damn thing about it. Not the players, not the leadership, and surely not all the ways they supposedly grew the game while merely growing their own bank accounts.

#6665. Margot Robbie’s Side-Parted ‘Buttercup Bob’ Is Polished and Easy to Wear: How to Try It Now

The actor and producer proves how a simple styling switch can make all the difference.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

There are few hair updates as simple, yet as impactful, as switching up your part. After several seasons dominated by center-parted lengths and deliberately undone styling, the side part is making a convincing return. The latest to make a case for its appeal? None other than Margot Robbie.

Attending Chanel’s Cruise 2026/2027 show in Australia, the actor and producer stepped out with a softly structured ‘buttercup bob,’ styled with a deep side part and flipped-out ends. The result was equal parts polished and quietly sophisticated. And while Robbie has recently been favoring the waterfall bob, this latest iteration feels notably sleeker, offering a more refined take on the trending cut.

“This bob is an effortless take on a classic jaw-length cut. It’s neat without feeling rigid, with soft ends and a clean shape that gives hair an expensive-looking finish,” Luke Hersheson, hairstylist and CEO of Hershesons previously told ELLE. In short, it trades lived-in texture for a more polished silhouette, with precise ends and subtle flicks upward that create a sharper finish.

Robbie’s latest style embodies the look perfectly, pairing a side part with softly sculpted ends for an updated take on the preppy bob. The part allows for a sweeping side bang, adding a touch of glamour to an otherwise classic cut.

When it comes to re-creating Robbie’s look, Talia Hurley, a stylist at Blue Tit London, recommends blow-drying the hair with a round brush for a glossy, classic finish. This will also help create added volume and the bend needed to achieve the look’s side-swept shape.

Luke Hersheson recommends finishing the look with a pea-sized amount of smoothing cream to add shine and tame flyaways without leaving the hair greasy, ensuring the style retains its polished, glossy finish.

#6664. ‘DWTS’ Pro Jenna Johnson Reveals She Broke Her Nose Less Than 24 Hours Before Grammy Night

Just weeks after Ezra Sosa nearly broke his nose while practicing with partner Amber Glenn, another Dancing With the Stars pro is dealing with a similar injury…except with an actual fracture this time. On November 10, just hours before the premiere of Grammy Night on DWTS season 35, Jenna Johnson took to social media to share the bad news.

According to Jenna (who is partnered with Harry Shum Jr. this season), the incident took place during camera blocking the night before. “So last night I definitely broke my nose during camera blocking,” she explained, pointing to the swelling in her face.

She also captioned the post, “Salsa: 1. Jenna’s Nose: 0.” But here’s what fans really want to know: Is Jenna still dancing tonight despite her injury?

While she didn’t explicitly say whether or not she’s still dancing, Jenna did add “Happy Show Day” to her caption, so it seems the show will go on!

Oh, and Harry also took to her comments section to jokingly write, “WHO DID THIS TO YOU?? ….oh…sorry…BUT YOU ARE A TOTAL BADASS!!!! 💪🏼”

Luckily, Jenna only has to dance one number tonight, as she and Harry earned immunity from this week’s dance-off after scoring a 40/40 for their jazz performance to “Supercalifragilisticexpialidocious” from Mary Poppins. Instead of going head-to-head with another pairing, Harry and Jenna will automatically get dance-off bonus points added to their score.

And what is their routine this week, you ask? The pro and the Grey’s Anatomy star are tackling a salsa to “Die With a Smile” by Lady Gaga and Bruno Mars.

Here’s hoping that Jenna’s smile isn’t affected by her broken nose!!
